To the New Mexico Department of Health: 
I am a New Mexico resident in Santa Fe. I am writing to comment on proposed rule 7.35.3 NMAC ahead of the 
October 2, 2026 hearing. 
I am a long time advocate for the responsible use of psychedelics for both clinical and spiritual purposes. I have my 
PhD in Culture and Mental health from UC-Berkeley which explored the cultural methods of achieving altered, 
expanded states of consciousness. I made a film, called FROM SHOCK TO AWE, which followed two veteran 
couples as they sought healing via psychedelics. As well, I am on the board of Limina Foundation, 
I ask the department to make the following changes in the final rule. 
1. The property-owner statement, 7.35.3.11(B)(9). 
Delete paragraph (B)(9) of 7.35.3.11. If the department wants documentation of a home setting, follow Colorado 
here: the patient documents their own legal right to occupy the residence, no owner signs anything, and the clinician 
brings only the session dose and removes anything unused the same day. 
The landlord interest that justifies the cannabis provision, smoking on the property, is not present in a supervised 
psilocybin session administered by a licensed facilitator. A signed acknowledgment gives an owner nothing to gain 
and something to lose, so many will decline to sign, and care at home for anyone who does not own their home will 
end before they are able to receive treatment. 
2. The certifying clinician, 7.35.3.8 and 7.35.3.9(D). 
Remove the certifying clinician design. In its place, regulate around the specific contraindications, medical histories 
and medications that warrant clinical review. Where a risk factor is identified, require an attestation from a clinician 
whose scope of practice fits that risk that screening was conducted and benefits outweigh risks. 
The design exceeds the Act and misunderstands where the risk is. The most significant risks in psilocybin-assisted 
therapy are psychological, or medical risks that must be screened on the day of administration, such as intoxication. 
A one-time “prescriber” gateway misses them. It also creates a clinical encounter whose only purpose is program 
entry, which insurers will treat as lacking independent medical necessity, inviting claim denials and clawbacks. 
3. The patient application, 7.35.3.8. 
Strike the patient application process. 
It has no legal or policy justification in a supervised-use program. It adds administrative burden for the department, 
lengthens the wait for care, creates another store of patient data that can be accessed without consent or unlawfully 
taken, and further stigmatizes patients. 
Thank you for considering my comment. 
Janine Sagert 
Santa Fe, New Mexico

To the New Mexico Department of Health: 
I am a New Mexico resident in Santa Fe, and I am also a renter. I am writing to comment on 
proposed rule 7.35.3 NMAC ahead of the October 2, 2026 hearing. 
It is deeply important to me that the program lands well, reaches people and delivers positive 
outcomes. Breaking the intended access and equity goals due to unjustified regulation will 
undo much of the hard work that the department has done to put this together. Below are 
solutions. 
I ask the department to make the following changes in the final rule. 
1. The property-owner statement, 7.35.3.11(B)(9). 
I ask the department to consider these solutions: 
a) Add to paragraph (B)(9) of 7.35.3.11: “This provision shall not apply to end-of-life 
patients or others seeking in-home care, or to long-term care facility residents.” 
OR 
b) Delete paragraph (B)(9) of 7.35.3.11. If the department wants documentation of a 
home setting, follow Colorado here: the patient documents their own legal right to 
occupy the residence, no owner signs anything, and the clinician brings only the session 
dose and removes anything unused the same day. 
The landlord interest that justifies the cannabis provision, smoking on the property, is not 
present in a supervised psilocybin session administered by a licensed facilitator. Federal 
illegality alone does not justify it either: New Mexico has already chosen to depart from 
federal law on this. Giving landlords the power to block care for vulnerable and disabled 
patients is contrary to that choice, and leaves the department exposed under state protections 
against discrimination. A signed acknowledgment gives an owner nothing to gain and 
something to lose, so many will decline to sign, and care at home for anyone who does not 
own their home will end before they are able to receive treatment. 
2. The certifying clinician, 7.35.3.8 and 7.35.3.9(D). 
Remove the certifying clinician design. In its place, regulate around the specific 
contraindications, medical histories and medications that warrant clinical review. Where a risk 
factor is identified, require an attestation from a clinician whose scope of practice fits that risk 
that screening was conducted and benefits outweigh risks. 
The design exceeds the Act and misunderstands where the risk is. The most significant risks in 
psilocybin-assisted therapy are psychological, or medical risks that must be screened on the 
day of administration, such as intoxication. A one-time “prescriber” gateway misses them. It 
also creates a clinical encounter whose only purpose is program entry, which insurers will 
treat as lacking independent medical necessity, inviting claim denials and clawbacks. 
Exhibit 8.DD.

===== PAGE 109 =====
3. The patient application, 7.35.3.8. 
Strike the patient application process. 
It has no legal or policy justification in a supervised-use program. It adds administrative 
burden for the department, lengthens the wait for care, creates another store of patient data that 
can be accessed without consent or unlawfully taken, and further stigmatizes patients. 
Thank you for considering my comment. 
Gregory L Evans 
Santa Fe, New Mexico

From: 
Dr. Scott Shannon

Subject: 
[EXTERNAL] Psilocybin program. 
Date: 
Tuesday, September 29, 2026 10:50:53 AM 
You don't often get email from scott.shannon@wholeness.com. Learn why this is important 
CAUTION: This email originated outside of our organization. Exercise caution prior to

Dear Mr. Clark, 
I am writing regarding the proposed rules for New Mexico’s Medical Psilocybin Program. I 
am a psychiatrist and psychedelic researcher in Colorado, where our clinic has extensive 
experience with ketamine-assisted therapy and regulated psilocybin services. I have authored 
many publications in this arena and frequently speak to professional audiences on these exact 
topics. 
I strongly support New Mexico’s effort to create a medically responsible psilocybin program. I 
am concerned, however, that two proposed requirements may add substantial cost and delay 
without meaningfully improving patient safety. 
First, requiring every patient to obtain an in-person certification from a clinician with a New 
Mexico controlled-substance registration appears unnecessarily restrictive. The Medical 
Psilocybin Act defines a qualified patient in relation to a clinician’s judgment that the person 
is medically appropriate based on a qualifying diagnosis, and the statute itself does not appear 
to make prescriptive authority the central clinical safeguard. The data coming out of Oregon’s 
model demonstrates an inordinately low adverse event rate without any restrictions of this 
kind. From a medical/psychiatric viewpoint the additional certification is simply not needed. 
This added layer will of course add cost to the system and reduce access. 
For psilocybin, the more important safety assessment occurs during preparation and 
immediately before administration. That assessment needs to consider current medications, 
cardiovascular status, psychiatric history, changes in health, and other risk factors that may 
affect whether treatment should proceed. The practitioner who prepares the patient and will be 
present for treatment is often in the best position to make that assessment. 
A more proportionate model would allow an appropriately licensed practitioner to document 
the qualifying diagnosis and require consultation or medical clearance from a physician or 
other prescriber when defined risk factors are present—for example, significant cardiovascular 
disease, uncontrolled hypertension, complex medication interactions, or a history suggesting 
elevated psychiatric risk. This concentrates medical resources where they actually improve 
safety rather than requiring an additional evaluation for every patient. 
Second, I would reconsider requiring a separate Department of Health approval before 
treatment can begin. If a qualified clinician has already determined that the patient meets the 
statutory requirements, an additional administrative review by individuals who have not 
examined the patient adds little clinical information. A potential delay of several weeks can be 
particularly consequential for patients with treatment-resistant depression or those receiving 
end-of-life care. This will needly restrict volume and access to care. Our experience in 
Colorado and those in Oregon highlight the viability of a clinical program without this 
Exhibit 8.EE.

===== PAGE 111 =====
 
restriction and volumes have been much lower than anticipated in both programs, amplifying 
the concern over access. 
A notification or registration model would accomplish the Department’s legitimate oversight 
and data-collection goals without making DOH approval another clinical gate. The 
Department could retain strong authority to establish standards, audit records, monitor 
outcomes, investigate adverse events, and enforce compliance. 
These distinctions are particularly important because psilocybin under this program is 
administered in an approved, supervised setting. The risk-management problem is therefore 
quite different from medical cannabis or another medication that patients take home and use 
independently. 
Good regulation should increase safety while keeping unnecessary barriers as low as possible. 
In my experience, safety in psychedelic medicine is best achieved through careful preparation, 
appropriate screening, skilled supervision, clear escalation criteria, and thoughtful follow-up— 
not by adding duplicative administrative checkpoints. 
I encourage the Department to reconsider these two provisions before finalizing the rules. 
Thank you for considering these comments. 
Sincerely, 
Scott Shannon, MD 
Psychiatrist and Psychedelic Researcher 
Colorado 
Scott Shannon, MD 
scott.shannon@wholeness.com

September 29, 2026 
 
End-of-Life Considerations and Concerns in the Draft Licensing, Training and Education 
Rules for New Mexico’s Medical Psilocybin Program  
Dear Department of Health Staff, Rule-making Team, and Hearing Officer, 
End of Life Options New Mexico (EOLONM), a statewide nonprofit organization that provides 
education, information and support for all end-of-life care options, including medical aid in dying,  
writes to provide public comment on the draft rules pertaining to Licensure, Training, and Education 
for New Mexico’s Medical Psilocybin Program. Our comments focus on the unique needs of end-of-
life patients and sections where the draft rules do not adequately account for these needs. We are 
grateful for the opportunity to provide feedback on these proposed rules and are committed to the 
program’s success.  
NMAC 7.35.3.11 APPLICATION PROCESS FOR HEALING CENTERS AND 
OTHER APPROVED LOCATIONS 
 
EOLONM is concerned about the landlord permission requirement in the location approval process 
and requests a clarifying amendment.  
The draft provision in question requires: 
“Proof of ownership by the qualified patient or attending practitioner or facilitator of 
any property on which medical psilocybin administration sessions will be conducted, 
or a signed, written statement from the owner of such property acknowledging that 
the owner understands that persons will be participating in the medical psilocybin 
program on the premises, and what those persons are authorized to do within the 
terms of their certification.” 
As written, this requirement applies to any property where an administration session occurs, including 
a patient’s own home. For end-of-life patients and other patients who receive in-home care and who 
rent rather than own their residence, the provision seems to require them to obtain a signed statement 
from their landlord acknowledging their participation in the Medical Psilocybin Program and 
Exhibit 8.GG.

===== PAGE 114 =====
describing what they are authorized to do under their certification, before they can receive care in their 
own home. 
We understand from MPA Advisory Board/Department discussions that this provision has origins 
from an analogous requirement within the Medical Cannabis Program. Cannabis is typically smoked 
or vaporized, and a landlord has a real, practical interest in knowing whether that will occur on their 
property, given the potential effects on odor, ventilation, and property value. Medical psilocybin 
administration is a different activity. It occurs during a discrete, clinically supervised session 
administered by a licensed facilitator, involves no combustion or lingering property effects, and is 
already governed by the program’s own supervision, screening, and safety requirements. The property-
owner interest that justifies the cannabis provision simply is not present here in the same way. 
What remains, for end-of-life and other patients seeking care in their rented home is the disclosure 
itself. The requirement effectively conditions a terminally ill or homebound person’s access to an in-
home medical psilocybin treatment on first disclosing their participation in a state medical program 
— and the general nature of a private medical condition — to their landlord, a third party with no 
clinical or regulatory role in their care. That is a forced disclosure of private health information to 
someone outside the treatment relationship, unsupported by any compelling public policy or private 
interest that this disclosure serves. It does not appear necessary for patient safety, for facilitator 
oversight, or for program integrity, all of which are already addressed elsewhere in the draft regulations 
through facilitator licensure, supervision requirements, and program registration. For a population 
that is often already navigating precarious housing, serious illness, and limited mobility, asking them 
to additionally negotiate this disclosure with a landlord is a significant and, we believe, unintended 
burden. 
Residents of long-term care facilities raise a related but separate concern. Similarly, a patient, 
practitioner, or facilitator would not be the property owner. Facilities already allow physicians, nurses, 
and hospice staff to provide other forms of care in a resident’s room without seeking treatment-by-
treatment consent from ownership. Requiring a special permission specific to psilocybin, and not to 
comparable medical treatments, singles out participants in the program for a disclosure and approval 
burden that does not appear tied to any actual safety or oversight need. 
Given that psilocybin ingestion does not have any property impact, we do not believe the property 
ownership provision is necessary in the context of medical psilocybin. However, if the Department 
wishes to retain the provision, we believe there needs to be an exemption to address the above 
concerns:   
“This provision shall not apply to end-of-life patients or others seeking in-home care, or to long-term 
care facility residents.” 

===== PAGE 115 =====
This language would preserve the underlying property-owner protection where it serves a real purpose, 
while relieving the state’s most vulnerable patients of a disclosure obligation that does not appear to 
advance any safety or oversight goal. We would be glad to discuss this further, to provide any 
additional information the Department finds useful, or to work with staff on alternative phrasing that 
achieves the same result. 
NMAC 7.35.3.13 REQUIREMENTS AND PROHIBITIONS FOR 
CERTIFYING CLINICIANS, PRACTITIONERS, AND FACILITATORS 
The proposed “certifying clinician” program design introduces a unique access barrier for end-of-life 
patients. The design would require all program patients to see a DOH-certified provider to confirm 
their qualifying condition and conduct a health screening, with this exam required in person except in 
narrow circumstances. Most end-of-life patients already have a well-established medical care team — 
the physicians best positioned to assess the patient’s medical prognosis and the medical 
appropriateness of psilocybin for that patient — but these physicians are unlikely to also be DOH-
certified. Requiring an end-of-life patient to locate an additional, DOH-certified physician simply to 
confirm the imminence of their own death asks these patients to spend time and energy they do not 
have.  
The safety motives presumed behind the “certifying clinician” design can be accomplished by other 
means that better account for the access needs of end-of-life and other program patients. Appropriate 
health and safety screening can be accomplished by permitted practitioners in accordance with the 
subject matter scope of practice of their underlying healthcare license. Practitioners overseeing 
patients with risks that exceed their subject matter scope of practice should consult with appropriate 
experts or refer patients out for additional health screening.  
We believe the entry design should be reworked, but if the Department decides to continue with the 
"certifying clinician” design, we request an exception from this design for end-of-life patients, allowing 
patients with a prognosis of fewer than 9 months to live to provide physician documentation of the 
prognosis to gain program entry.  
NMAC 7.35.3.8 PATIENT ENROLLMENT APPLICATION PROCESS  
The proposed patient application process in the draft rules should be reconsidered. For patients facing 
terminal or life-limiting diagnoses, every added administrative step in the treatment process is time 
and energy they may not have. An application-and-approval process introduces delay into care that 
should be available as quickly as clinically appropriate. It may take some time for the certifying clinician 
to assemble and submit the required documentation for the application, and then the rules reserve 30 
days for the department to review the application. This could add additional months in just 
administrative time to the care, which for end-of-life patients could mean the difference between 
receiving relief or dying before it is available. We respectfully request that the Department reconsider 

===== PAGE 116 =====
the design.  
The application design appears to be a feature borrowed from the Medical Cannabis program 
structure. The “patient identification card” model in the Cannabis program exists because authorized 
patients possess, outside of any supervised setting, what would otherwise be an illegal substance. The 
Lynn and Erin Compassionate Use Act built a patient application process specifically to manage that 
unsupervised possession risk, but the medical psilocybin program presents no such risk. Because it 
operates on a model of supervised administration at approved locations only, no patient will ever 
possess psilocybin outside of a supervised episode. There is no scenario requiring a patient to prove 
authorization to possess the substance, and therefore no justification for requiring an application or 
identification card before an end-of-life patient can access care. 
For patients who may have weeks or months, not years, the application process will cause further 
delay between diagnosis and treatment, and for end-of-life patients, this concern is especially acute. 
We recommend the application process be removed.  
We appreciate the opportunity to provide these comments and concerns. People near their end of life 
have unique challenges that access to medical psilocybin may be helpful with.  However, they must be 
able to obtain this assistance in a manner that acknowledges and is responsive to their physical and 
emotional frailty. Thanks for your consideration of these recommendations and we look forward to 
continuing to work with the Department to create and implement this unique and important new 
program. 
Sincerely, 
 
Julia Bergen, Executive Director 
End of Life Options New Mexico 
 
 
Marisa Bartolotta 
Compassion & Choices 
Policy Counsel

Dear Mr. Clark, 
I am writing in regards New Mexico's medical psilocybin program hearing on October 2nd. I 
am a natural medicine facilitator in the state of Colorado, an Army veteran and a former 
resident of New Mexico. I am also a person who has healed significantly from the use of 
Psilocybin therapy. As a veteran, I use the VA for all of my health insurance. My 
breakthrough moments of healing would not be possible or very hard to achieve, if I were to 
instead rely on the VA in NM to approve my psilocybin facilitation. As you know, the VA 
uses federal laws and the new law passed for the psilocybin care would not be possible 
through the VA or very rare with very many hoops to jump through to be approved. I am not 
able to afford health care through private insurance and it would not be financially viable for 
me to seek out a private clinician to approve my work with psilocybin in addition to the fees 
for the therapy. For someone who is seeking deep care and healing, the price of the session is 
something alone they must save for and consider. If the criteria for approving therapy is 
controlled as it is currently outlined in the regulations, very few people will receive help or 
even bother to go through this process. This program is important for people who want to try 
this therapy in a safe, supportive and legal environment. Without approval people may instead 
find medicine that has not been tested, or go to another state when the money could be spent in 
New Mexico. 
The training for a natural medicine facilitator is intensive. It covers ethics, cultural awareness, 
medication, referring clients, working with clients under a variety of emotions and ailments 
and so many other important topics. These facilitators are trained and ready to help others. We 
are unique in that we want to help others grow and heal. We want to sit with them through 
their hardships and help them recover and contribute more to their communities, families and 
themselves. 
This is true healing work that will impact the state of New Mexico positively one person at a 
time. Please consider making this step easier for people. 
Thank you, 
Sara Petrous

From: 
Catherine Warnock 
To: 
Clark, Jacob, DOH 
Subject: 
[EXTERNAL] Public Comment on Training Accessibility and Workforce Development, Proposed Rule 7.35.3 NMAC 
Date: 
Wednesday, September 30, 2026 8:20:57 PM 
You don't often get email from catherine@mariposacounselingcenter.com. Learn why this is important 
CAUTION: This email originated outside of our organization. Exercise caution prior to 
clicking on links or opening attachments. 
To the New Mexico Department of Health: 
I am writing to express concern about the training and practicum requirements contained in 
proposed Rule 7.35.3 NMAC and their potential impact on affordability and workforce 
development. 
I strongly support rigorous training for psilocybin practitioners and facilitators. As someone 
who has provided psilocybin-assisted therapy education and training in New Mexico for 
several years, I understand firsthand the importance of adequate education, supervised 
experience, and demonstrated competency. 
My concern is not with high standards. It is with whether the proposed structure is the most 
practical and affordable way to achieve them. 
The proposed rules require substantial didactic and simulated-patient education followed by a 
100-hour practicum for facilitators and a 120-hour practicum for practitioners. The practicum 
also requires specific experiences involving individual and group administration, preparation 
and integration, multiple patients, and different qualifying conditions. NMAC-7.35.3­
NewRule NMAC-7.35.3-NewRule 
These requirements create costs on both sides of the training equation. 
Students must be able to afford extensive education and supervised practicum hours. At the 
same time, educational programs must develop the personnel, patient population, approved 
locations, supervision, group experiences, and administrative infrastructure necessary to 
provide those hours. 
This creates a significant startup challenge for New Mexico. 
Small, locally owned educational programs may simply not have the capital or infrastructure 
to provide this level of practicum initially. Large universities, healthcare systems, national 
training organizations, and well-capitalized companies are much better positioned to absorb 
those costs. 
I am concerned that New Mexico could unintentionally create a training system in which 
financial capacity, rather than educational quality and demonstrated competency, 
determines which organizations are able to train our workforce. 
There is also a fundamental workforce-development problem. New Mexico needs trained 
practitioners and facilitators in order to launch a successful program. Yet educational 
programs will need patients, approved treatment locations, qualified supervisors, facilitators, 
and sufficient individual and group administration sessions in order to train those practitioners. 
Exhibit 8.OO.

===== PAGE 128 =====
In other words, the workforce needed to make the program operational depends upon a 
practicum infrastructure that may not fully exist until the program is already 
operational. 
This could create a bottleneck precisely when New Mexico needs to rapidly and responsibly 
develop its workforce. 
The cost will ultimately affect patients as well. Training expenses become part of the cost of 
entering professional practice, and practitioners must eventually recover those expenses 
through the services they provide. An unnecessarily expensive workforce-development model 
therefore contributes to an unnecessarily expensive treatment system. 
I encourage the Department to reconsider whether competency could be demonstrated through 
a more flexible practicum model rather than relying primarily on a prescribed number of hours 
and specific treatment encounters. 
I also encourage consideration of: 
A phased practicum requirement during the initial implementation years 
Competency-based pathways that recognize demonstrated skills and prior relevant 
experience 
Greater flexibility in how required clinical experiences are obtained 
State-supported scholarships or workforce-development funding for New Mexico 
residents 
Grants or startup assistance for small New Mexico-based educational programs 
Periodic review of the training requirements based on actual workforce capacity, 
training costs, and patient access after implementation 
New Mexico should absolutely establish high standards for those entrusted with this work. But 
high standards do not have to mean high financial barriers. 
If we want a diverse workforce that includes community-based clinicians, rural providers, 
peer-support professionals, and experienced New Mexico practitioners, we must design a 
training pathway they can realistically enter and educational programs that local organizations 
can realistically provide. 
Otherwise, we risk creating a program that is technically available but dependent upon large 
institutions and financially inaccessible to many of the very New Mexicans we need to build 
it. 
Thank you for considering these comments. 
Catherine Warnock, MA, LPCC, NCC 
Las Cruces, New Mexico

Mariposa Counseling Center 
www.mariposacounselingcenter.com 
www.catherinewarnock.com

From: 
Amy Wong Hope 
To: 
Clark, Jacob, DOH 
Cc: 
Amy Wong Hope 
Subject: 
[EXTERNAL] Public comment on proposed rule 7.35.3 NMAC, hearing of October 2, 2026 
Date: 
Wednesday, September 30, 2026 9:04:54 PM 
You don't often get email from awhope@resilientwithin.com. Learn why this is important 
CAUTION: This email originated outside of our organization. Exercise caution prior to 
clicking on links or opening attachments. 
Dear Jacob, 
I am a New Mexico resident submitting public comment on the proposed rules for the Medical 
Psilocybin Act. I am a Licensed Clinical Social Worker in New Mexico and Colorado, a Licensed 
Natural Medicine Clinical Facilitator in Colorado, and the founder of the Psychedelic Studies 
Certificate Program at Southwestern College School of Counseling in Santa Fe. 
My comments are informed by my experience as a mental health clinician and by my direct 
experience working within Colorado’s regulated natural medicine program. I respectfully request 
that the Department consider the following changes to the proposed rules. 
1. Certifying Clinician — 7.35.3.8 and 7.35.3.9(D) 
I recommend revising these provisions so that, for qualifying mental health conditions, certification 
may be provided by a licensed mental health professional whose scope of practice includes 
diagnosis and assessment of those conditions, rather than requiring certification by a medical 
prescriber. 
There are several reasons for this recommendation: 
First, the proposed requirement may create an unnecessary barrier to patient access. 
Requiring a patient who already has an established mental health provider to locate a separate 
medical prescriber willing to conduct a certification evaluation adds another step, additional 
expense, and potentially significant delays in accessing the program. 
Second, medical prescribers may be reluctant to assume this role. Medical prescribers with 
whom I have spoken have expressed concern about assuming responsibility for certifying a 
patient following what may be a single diagnostic encounter, particularly when they are not 
providing the patient’s ongoing mental health treatment. Several have indicated that they would be 
unlikely to participate in the program for this reason. If this experience is widespread, the 
requirement could substantially limit the number of clinicians willing to provide certifications. 
Third, licensed mental health professionals already diagnose and assess mental health 
conditions within their professional scopes of practice. Licensed mental health clinicians 
complete graduate-level education, supervised clinical practice, licensure examinations, 
continuing education, and ongoing professional regulation. Their clinical work routinely includes 
assessment, diagnosis, differential diagnosis, risk assessment, and treatment of mental health 
Exhibit 8.PP.

===== PAGE 131 =====
conditions. 
For qualifying psychiatric conditions, these clinicians are therefore well positioned to determine 
whether a patient meets the diagnostic criteria required by the Medical Psilocybin Act. 
It is also important, however, to distinguish eligibility for the program from clinical readiness 
and safety for a psilocybin administration session. A determination that a patient has a 
qualifying diagnosis should not itself be interpreted as a determination that psilocybin 
administration is safe or clinically appropriate for that individual at that particular time. 
A patient may meet criteria for a qualifying condition while also having co-occurring psychiatric 
conditions, current symptoms, medications, psychosocial instability, or other factors that require 
additional assessment before proceeding. Some of these concerns may not be apparent during a 
single certification visit. 
For this reason, I recommend that the rules clearly distinguish between: 
1. certification that the patient meets the statutory criteria for participation in the Medical 
Psilocybin Program; and 
2. the subsequent assessment of whether that individual can safely and appropriately 
participate in a psilocybin administration session. 
Without this distinction, patients may understandably interpret certification as a clinical “green 
light” to proceed. Facilitators may likewise place inappropriate reliance on the certification when 
making their own safety determinations. 
In Colorado, my responsibilities as a Licensed Natural Medicine Clinical Facilitator include 
assessing whether consultation with other members of a client's health care team is necessary 
before proceeding. When medical or pharmacological issues fall outside my professional scope, I 
seek appropriate consultation. In my own practice, I also consult with a PhD-level pharmacologist 
when medication interactions, contraindications, or other pharmacological concerns require 
specialized expertise. 
I recommend that New Mexico adopt a similarly interdisciplinary approach: when a patient's 
medical needs fall outside the scope of the evaluating mental health professional or facilitator, 
consultation with an appropriately qualified medical professional should be required or obtained as 
clinically indicated. 
Finally, I recommend that the Department establish clear screening and safety standards 
addressing conditions and histories associated with increased risk during psilocybin 
administration, including bipolar-spectrum conditions, histories of psychosis, and other clinically 
significant risk factors. Rather than treating every such factor as an automatic exclusion, the rules 
should clearly identify conditions requiring exclusion, additional evaluation, medical or psychiatric 
consultation, or heightened clinical consideration. 
2. Patient Application — 7.35.3.8 

===== PAGE 132 =====
I recommend eliminating the separate patient application process and instead allowing patient 
enrollment to occur through the certification process. 
The certifying clinician could submit the minimum information necessary to establish program 
eligibility, while maintaining the patient's underlying clinical records in accordance with applicable 
professional recordkeeping and privacy requirements. 
This approach would have several advantages. 
It would reduce the administrative burden on patients, particularly patients who are seriously ill, 
disabled, experiencing significant psychiatric symptoms, or receiving end-of-life care. It would also 
reduce the administrative infrastructure required of the Department. 
More importantly, the Department should consider the principle of data minimization when 
determining what patient information it needs to collect and retain. Psilocybin treatment may 
involve highly sensitive medical and mental health information. Every additional repository 
containing identifiable patient information creates an additional privacy and data-security 
exposure. 
I therefore recommend that the Department collect and retain only the minimum patient 
information necessary to administer the Medical Psilocybin Program, while leaving detailed clinical 
records with the licensed professionals responsible for maintaining them. 
3. Property-Owner Statement — 7.35.3.11(B)(9) 
I recommend deleting paragraph (B)(9) of 7.35.3.11 or, alternatively, adding the following 
exemption: 
“This provision shall not apply to end-of-life patients or other patients receiving in-home 
care, or to residents of long-term care facilities.” 
The proposed property-owner requirement creates a particularly significant access issue for 
patients receiving end-of-life care. 
A person with a life-threatening illness may wish to receive psilocybin services in their own home 
because illness, disability, pain, fatigue, mobility limitations, or the nature of end-of-life care makes 
travel to another location difficult or inappropriate. A patient who rents their home should not face 
an additional barrier to receiving otherwise lawful health care solely because they do not own the 
property in which they live. 
We do not ordinarily expect a landlord to make clinical decisions regarding whether a tenant may 
receive hospice, psychotherapy, physical therapy, nursing, or other lawful health services in the 
tenant's home. Requiring property-owner authorization for psilocybin services could create a 
substantially different standard for patients participating in this program. 
At minimum, the Department should create an exemption for end-of-life patients, patients 

===== PAGE 133 =====
receiving medically necessary in-home care, and residents of long-term care settings. 
4. Education Requirements — Touch and Consent 
The proposed educational requirements include “Touch and Somatic Awareness.” I recommend 
that this language explicitly include touch consent, for example: 
“Touch, Somatic Awareness, and Touch Consent.” 
Consent surrounding touch deserves explicit treatment in facilitator education rather than being 
presumed to fall within general instruction regarding somatic awareness. 
Psilocybin can place patients in unusually vulnerable psychological and interpersonal states. 
Facilitators therefore need specific training regarding obtaining consent for touch, maintaining 
boundaries, recognizing withdrawal or changes in consent, and understanding that consent given 
before a session does not eliminate the need to attend to consent during the session. 
5. Permissible Touch Between Facilitator and Patient 
I also recommend that the Department establish explicit standards defining permissible and 
prohibited touch between a facilitator and patient during psilocybin services. 
Colorado and Oregon have established regulatory boundaries concerning facilitator-client touch. 
New Mexico should likewise provide facilitators and patients with clear standards rather than 
leaving the boundaries of permissible touch ambiguous. 
The rules should identify the areas of the body where supportive touch may be permitted, 
establish that such touch requires the patient's prior consent, clarify that consent may be 
withdrawn at any time, and explicitly prohibit sexualized or otherwise inappropriate touch. 
Clear regulatory boundaries protect both patients and facilitators. They are particularly important 
in psychedelic care because patients may experience increased suggestibility, emotional 
openness, altered perception, and vulnerability during administration. 
For these reasons, I respectfully ask the Department to incorporate explicit touch-consent 
education into facilitator training requirements and to establish clear standards governing 
permissible touch during psilocybin services. 
Conclusion 
I support the development of a Medical Psilocybin Program that is clinically responsible, 
accessible, trauma-informed, and protective of patients. My recommendations are intended to 
reduce unnecessary barriers to care while strengthening clinical assessment, informed consent, 
professional accountability, privacy, and patient safety. 
Thank you for considering these comments as the Department finalizes the regulations 
implementing New Mexico's Medical Psilocybin Act. 

===== PAGE 134 =====
 
 
 
 
 
Respectfully, 
Amy Wong Hope, LCSW 
Licensed Clinical Social Worker, New Mexico and Colorado 
Licensed Natural Medicine Clinical Facilitator, Colorado 
Founder, Psychedelic Studies Certificate Program 
Southwestern College School of Counseling

To the New Mexico Department of Health: 
I am a New Mexico resident in Santa Fe, and I am also a clinician in training. I am writing to 
comment on proposed rule 7.35.3 NMAC ahead of the October 2, 2026 hearing. 
I ask the department to make the following changes in the final rule. 
1. The property-owner statement, 7.35.3.11(B)(9). 
Delete paragraph (B)(9) of 7.35.3.11. If the department wants documentation of a home 
setting, follow Colorado here: the patient documents their own legal right to occupy the 
residence, no owner signs anything, and the clinician brings only the session dose and removes 
anything unused the same day. 
The landlord interest that justifies the cannabis provision, smoking on the property, is not 
present in a supervised psilocybin session administered by a licensed facilitator. A signed 
acknowledgment gives an owner nothing to gain and something to lose, so many will decline 
to sign, and care at home for anyone who does not own their home will end before they are 
able to receive treatment. 
2. The certifying clinician, 7.35.3.8 and 7.35.3.9(D). 
Remove the certifying clinician design. In its place, regulate around the specific 
contraindications, medical histories and medications that warrant clinical review. Where a risk 
factor is identified, require an attestation from a clinician whose scope of practice fits that risk 
that screening was conducted and benefits outweigh risks. 
The design exceeds the Act and misunderstands where the risk is. The most significant risks in 
psilocybin-assisted therapy are psychological, or medical risks that must be screened on the 
day of administration, such as intoxication. A one-time “prescriber” gateway misses them. It 
also creates a clinical encounter whose only purpose is program entry, which insurers will 
treat as lacking independent medical necessity, inviting claim denials and clawbacks. 
3. The patient application, 7.35.3.8. 
Strike the patient application process. 
It has no legal or policy justification in a supervised-use program. It adds administrative 
burden for the department, lengthens the wait for care, creates another store of patient data that 
can be accessed without consent or unlawfully taken, and further stigmatizes patients. 
Thank you for considering my comment. I have personally experienced the healing properties 
of psilocybin and I believe it will be a great asset to the mental health community if it’s patient 
accessibility is adequate. 
Cynthia Gamel 
Clinical Counseling Practicum Student

You don't often get email from mary.curry1111@gmail.com. Learn why this is important

To the New Mexico Department of Health: 
I am a New Mexico resident in Santa Fe, and I am also a clinician. I am writing to comment 
on proposed rule 7.35.3 NMAC ahead of the October 2, 2026 hearing. 
I specialize in the treatment of traumatic stress, and there is a preponderance of evidence based 
practice and practice based evidence that full dose treatment and microdose treatment can 
attenuate suffering and result in a cure for PTSD and other chronic and debilitating 
psychological conditions. 
I plan to attend the hearing. If the opportunity to make a public statement, I will speak to my 
position that an Integrated Behavioral Heath and Primary Care Coordinated Model is the ideal 
model for the New Mexico Psilocybin Program. 
I ask the department to make the following changes in the final rule. 
1. The property-owner statement, 7.35.3.11(B)(9). 
Add to paragraph (B)(9) of 7.35.3.11: “This provision shall not apply to end-of-life patients or 
others seeking in-home care, or to long-term care facility residents.” 
The landlord interest that justifies the cannabis provision, smoking on the property, is not 
present in a supervised psilocybin session administered by a licensed facilitator. Federal 
illegality alone does not justify it either: New Mexico has already chosen to depart from 
federal law on this. Giving landlords the power to block care for vulnerable and disabled 
patients is contrary to that choice, and leaves the department exposed under state protections 
against discrimination. 
2. The certifying clinician, 7.35.3.8 and 7.35.3.9(D). 
Remove the certifying clinician design. In its place, regulate around the specific 
contraindications, medical histories and medications that warrant clinical review. Where a risk 
factor is identified, require an attestation from a clinician whose scope of practice fits that risk 
that screening was conducted and benefits outweigh risks. 
The design exceeds the Act and misunderstands where the risk is. The most significant risks in 
psilocybin-assisted therapy are psychological, or medical risks that must be screened on the 
day of administration, such as intoxication. A one-time “prescriber” gateway misses them. It 
also creates a clinical encounter whose only purpose is program entry, which insurers will 
treat as lacking independent medical necessity, inviting claim denials and clawbacks. 
Instead, I propose an Integrated Mental Health and Substance Abuse and Medical model that 
is Client/Patient centered. 
The entry point of screening and diagnosis is a licensed mental health professional that has 
Exhibit 8.UU.

===== PAGE 143 =====
 
completed a certification to provide psilocybin treatment. 
Utilize a screening tool that identifies the psychological and medical conditions that are 
considered counter indications. If a medical condition ( e.g. high blood pressure or other heart 
condition) the client/patient is responsible for meeting with their physician for clearance to 
proceed with treatment. 
Integrated models have been very successful in New Mexico. I was part of the team that 
designed the Statewide HIV/AIDS integrated model and the Statewide SBIRT model. These 
two models utilized Behavioral Health as the point of entry. 
3. The patient application, 7.35.3.8. 
Strike the patient application process. 
It has no legal or policy justification in a supervised-use program. It adds administrative 
burden for the department, lengthens the wait for care, creates another store of patient data that 
can be accessed without consent or unlawfully taken, and further stigmatizes patients. 
Instead, the eligible client/patient process occurs with a certified psilocybin clinician, either 
directly with their own client/patient, or in collaboration with a referring clinician or medical 
provider. 
Thank you for considering my comment. 
Dr. Mary Curry 
505-670-8797 
Santa Fe, New Mexico

entry, which insurers will treat as lacking independent medical necessity, inviting claim denials and clawbacks.

lengthens the wait for care, creates another store of patient data that can be accessed without consent or unlawfully 
taken, and further stigmatizes patients.

===== PAGE 149 =====
New Mexico’s Magic Mushroom Program Has a Math Problem 
The rules may protect patients while pricing the legal market out of 
existence 
By Wendy Robbins 
New Mexico is preparing to offer medical psilocybin for treatment-
resistant depression, PTSD, substance-use disorders and end-of-life 
care. This could change lives. 
Before we build an expensive new system around a mushroom, let us 
do some simple math. 
One patient would probably consume a macro dose of about 3 to 5 
grams during one treatment. One thousand patients would need 
only 6.6 to 11 pounds. Ten thousand patients would need only 66 
to 110 pounds for the entire year. 
That is roughly the weight of one large dog. One capable commercial 
grower could produce it. 
On today’s unregulated market, mushrooms commonly retail for $5 to 
$12 per gram. Those 66 to 110 pounds would be worth approximately 
$150,000 to $600,000. Now compare the mushrooms ’value with the 
bureaucratic machinery required to deliver them legally. 
The supervision: A full-dose session typically lasts six to eight hours 
and requires both a practitioner and facilitator. Using illustrative rates 
of $200 an hour for the practitioner and $100 for the facilitator, 10,000 
patients would require 20,000 six-to-eight-hour staff shifts—costing 
$18 million to $24 million for supervision alone. That excludes 
preparation, integration, mushrooms, administrative overhead, 
insurance and rent. 
The proposed training: Practitioners—already licensed healthcare 
professionals—would need roughly 200 hours of training; facilitators, 
roughly 180, plus a New Mexico module. No programs are approved 
yet. At Colorado’s rates, training 100 people could collectively cost 
$700,000 to $1.2 million—more than the mushrooms themselves. 
Exhibit 8.YY.

===== PAGE 150 =====
The rooms: Ten thousand individual treatments require 10,000 room-
days. Even if every room is used 250 days a year, New Mexico would 
need at least 40 treatment rooms operating continuously. Each needs 
secure storage, disability access, emergency plans, confidential 
records, insurance and staff. 
One Oregon operator expected to spend $10,000 on licensing, about 
$12,000 on insurance and thousands more on security—before paying 
rent, renovating or hiring anyone. Apparently, the mushroom needs an 
entourage and a building code. 
The testing: Each separate lot must be tested for potency and 
contaminants, with some testing repeated after five or six months. 
New Mexico has not published prices. Oregon’s only licensed 
psilocybin laboratory charges $600 for a compliance test. Fifty lots 
would cost $30,000 before retesting, while a laboratory may need 
hundreds of thousands of dollars in equipment to serve this tiny 
market. 
Remember: the mushroom is worth $15 to $60 per patient. 
Regulated experiences may begin around $1,500, while three- to five-
day retreats in Oregon and Colorado commonly cost $2,500 to $5,000 
or more. Those packages may include preparation, lodging, meals 
and some follow-up integration. 
These are package prices, not practitioner pay. Across 10,000 
patients, $1,500 to $5,000 each becomes $15 million to $50 million in 
charges. 
Oregon is already warning us. Its number of licensed service centers 
has fallen to about 24. Regulators have proposed doubling annual 
license fees to $20,000 for service centers and manufacturers and 
$4,000 for facilitators. Apparently, the cure for too few businesses is 
charging the survivors more. 
Colorado has tried to lower costs by allowing micro-healing centers 
within existing practices. It also allows adults to grow and share 
mushrooms personally. New Mexico offers neither escape valve. 

===== PAGE 151 =====
And then there is microdosing. Microdosing means taking a tiny 
dose for subtle effects without producing a psychedelic experience. 
The proposed rules provide no workable pathway. 
Imagine 10,000 people taking 0.25 gram three times weekly. That 
creates 30,000 doses every week and about 860 pounds of annual 
demand—recurring business that could help support growers and 
laboratories. 
But if every tiny dose requires an authorized session at an approved 
location, that means 1.56 million supervised appointments 
annually. There are not enough practitioners, facilitators, rooms or 
dollars. A microdose would require a macro-sized bureaucracy. 
Microdosing research remains incomplete. That is a reason to study it 
and create a cautious pathway—not to pretend people are not already 
using untested products. 
New Mexico is not facing a mushroom shortage. It is facing a 
math problem. Safety rules that make legal treatment unaffordable 
will send patients back to the unregulated market the rules were 
supposed to replace. 
The Department of Health will hold its rule hearing Friday, October 2, 
2026, at 9 a.m. Email written comments to jacob.clark@doh.nm.gov 
before the hearing closes. 
Simply write: I support safe, legal psilocybin treatment. Before 
adopting these rules, please publish a complete economic analysis, 
reduce unnecessary costs and create a workable pathway for 
microdosing. Please show New Mexicans how this program can 
survive financially. 
A program without affordable patients, viable laboratories, 
trained professionals or open clinics is not a medical program. It 
is an extremely well-regulated empty room. 

===== PAGE 152 =====

Public comment Regarding: PROHIBITION AGAINST DUAL OWNERSHIP IN 
CERTIFICANT AND PERMITTEE: 
A certificant or a person who holds an ownership interest in a certificant shall not hold an 
ownership interest in a permittee. 
[7.35.3.23 NMAC, xx/xx/2026] 
I am an Emergency Physician in rural northern New Mexico and also hold a certification in 
Psychedelic Assisted Therapy from Memoru (formerly Naropa University) in Colorado. One 
of my hopes to provide more affordable care was to have vertically integrated businesses. The 
first set of amendments clearly stated that all of the risk would fall back to the clinician. As I 
understand, you would have me take on the liability of bad outcomes but not allow me to grow 
the medicine that I will prescribe or possibly even curate the space in which to administer it 
(healing center)? Vertically integrating prescribing, growing and administering would 
substantially cut costs! Otherwise, you will have a bunch of people initiating a huge endeavor 
only to find it is not sustainable for them as individual businesses and the program will be too 
expensive/exclusive and therefore short-lived or ineffective. There are a lot of other examples 
of how you are making this program very expensive and/or inaccessible (landlord permission? 
in-person clinician visit!?! primary care clinics are shuttering doors in northern NM) but 
prohibiting vertical integration is the topic I wanted to address. Please reconsider. The vision 
is holistic. If I, the clinician, am to take on the risk of the bad outcomes, I want to be able to 
curate the medicine and the space that will provide the least likelihood of those occurrences in 
the first place. What's behind the first door? Another door. 
Thank you, 
Heather Krause, MD

Jacob,

Thank You. 
Jim Dodson / citizen researcher 
_____________________________ 
On this day thirteen years ago, I suffered an accidental blow to my prefrontal cortex that two 
years later led to a Social Security Judge declaring me fully and permanently disabled with 
MDD and PTSD. 
What medicine did not know at that time I learned ten years later as a citizen researcher. My 
brain, for efficiency, pruned back all the neural networks associated with the impact injury. 
Leaving a limited loop feedback network that made self-regulation impossible. And me a 
liability to any employer and to my family members. 
As life collapsed around me I was directed to a psychiatrist by my PCP, after completing a 
mood assessment form during an annual physical exam. 
After months of trying multiple options, my psychiatrist told me I was treatment resistant, as 
most PTSD patients are to traditional methods. 
It was difficult to hear I was hopelessly trapped in my own mind. My research led me to ask if 
what I was hearing about psychedelic therapy was true. That it was 80 % effective for the most 
severe patients. Yes, she said it was true. 
But while promising, it remained illegal here then and she could not prescribe it. But she 
would if she could, and her license couldn’t be revoked. 
So, I did what I had to do to save myself and procured my own medicine with a simple text 
request. This is life in New Mexico. I tested it first with a minimal dose by playing the best 
round of golf in my life. 
Clearly this was a performance enhancing medicine. I could see better than any human on that 
course in that moment. I felt surprisingly energized. 
The heroic dose that followed happened safely and quietly in the comfort of my own 
surroundings where I feel at peace. My own space. 
Meeting the mushroom one on one transformed me. I returned to my psychiatrist two weeks 
later to discuss the miracle I experienced. 
Exhibit 8.BBB.

===== PAGE 155 =====
Now I was treatment responsive and ready to discover how to be the me I was intended to be. 
My psychiatrist was so impressed with my progress she left her practice to join a psilocybin 
clinical program with the Veterans Administration. 
She handed me over to another remarkable psychiatrist who then opened my mind to Carl 
Jung and the journey to Individuation. An ideal pairing with the psychedelic journey. 
I consumed it with a passion and began my own journal, as Jung describes as an elevated 
inner-directed process he called, Active Imagination. That psychiatrist did something she says 
never happens. She dismissed me as self-regulating and told me I was taking up space from 
others who needed her more than I did. Didactical behavioral therapy put me on the path with 
heart. 
All of this treatment process, it is important to point out, exists today. Paid for by the existing 
health care industry standards. If only the medicine were simply prescribed like any other 
protected drug and used as instructed. New Mexico can do this the day the Advisory Board 
authorizes it because the law gives them that authority. 
This is the KISS option for treatment that is affordable and free of all the scare tactics to force 
traumatized patients into retreat settings with strangers for a fee far beyond the reach of most 
citizens in our state. 
It is overpriced snake oil that some wealthy patients might find more comfortable. But many 
New Mexico patients will never consider trusting Western medical providers under those 
conditions. Their family or a close friend can watch over them during the consumption, saving 
the patient thousands of dollars in over reach. 
When the patient feels safe, the medicine is at its best. Guides or Facilitators are useless 
voyeurs. Home settings must be permitted just as retreat settings are permitted. Both 
modalities accomplish the same result. There is no moral reason to exclude the sojourner 
pathway. Let the patient choose for themselves how to consume the medicine. 
I predict the affordable option will be very popular and a multitude will be healed quickly with 
the system already in place. 
Thousands have gone untreated in the five years since I paired up with my psychiatrist. It is 
time to open the flood gate to healing the masses. 
Then just a few months ago, my first psychiatrist contacted me again to follow up on my 
status. Now she is a professor at Emory University. And we are collaborating on a paper that 
shows that giving exclusive access to the retreat directed modality is a costly misstep. Because 
it reinvents the wheel that isn’t actually broken at all. 
I asked my psychiatrist to deliver my public comment on my behalf, for this reason. Patient 
voices go unheard in health care. Drowned out by data driven professionals that see them as an 
annoyance or profit center. 
But they will listen to my psychiatrist and professor at America’s premiere psychedelic 
research university. 
Hopefully the Advisory Board will then proceed to fix the rules they created to permit more 
patients and more inner-directed modalities to coexist. For the sake of the patients right to 
choose.

Comments on Proposed Rule by Henry T Harbin MD  
Submitted Oct 1,2026  
CHAPTER 35 MEDICAL PSILOCYBIN 
PART 3   PATIENTS, CERTIFYING CLINICIANS, PRACTITIONERS, FACILITATORS, 
HEALING CENTERS, OTHER APPROVED LOCATIONS, AND EDUCATIONAL 
PROGRAMS 
Henry Harbin, MD, is a psychiatrist with more than fifty years of extensive 
experience in the behavioral health field. He has served as CEO/ Chairman of 
two national behavioral health care companies: Magellan Health Services and 
Greenspring Health Services. At the time he was CEO of Magellan, it was the 
largest managed behavioral health care company in the U.S. managing the 
mental health and substance abuse benefits of approximately 70 million 
Americans. Dr. Harbin was also Director of the Mental Health Authority for the 
State of Maryland, member of the President’s New Freedom Commission on 
Mental Health, and co-chair of the National Business Group on Health’s 
workgroup to improve corporate employees’ behavioral health. Since 2004, 
he has provided health care consulting to numerous organizations, including 
AARP, CMMI, Dept of Labor, the Parity Implementation Coalition, the Kennedy 
Forum, the Path Forward for Mental Health and Substance Use. He is an 
adviser and board member of BrainFutures.    
   
BrainFutures (BF) has been active in supporting the development of the medical 
infrastructure for Psychedelics for over 5 years and Dr Harbin was a contributor to 
several of these Psychedelic Issue Briefs and Reports issued by BF on access, 
standards and reimbursement of Psychedelics.  
These comments on the proposed rules reﬂect my individual professional opinion 
and I am not representing BrainFutures in the below statements.     
 
I have reviewed the proposed regulations and I think the guidelines for the 
Certifying clinician are appropriate and are consistent with the types of medical 
screening that is required and implemented in the health care system today. As 
New Mexico is the ﬁrst state to implement a state wide Medical Access program 
Exhibit 8.CCC.

===== PAGE 157 =====
for psilocybin the need for careful medical screening for safety and eﬃcacy is 
paramount. As the New Mexico statute is explicit about the potential of psilocybin 
to treat serious mental health disorders there needs to be appropriate expertise 
of the professionals’ making diagnoses and have the knowledge to screen and 
monitor any side eﬀects or adverse events as well as to assist patients before, 
during and after their medication sessions.  
 In addition, many people who want to access these compounds will have co-
occurring medical and behavioral conditions and are likely to be on other medical 
or psychotropic medications necessitating medical screening.  
In reviewing the Practitioner requirements, it’s very positive that the regulations 
require a licensed professional: “documentation of current professional license to 
practice therapy, counseling, or behavioral services in New Mexico (e.g., PSY, LSW, 
LCSW)”.  In looking at the training requirements for the Practitioners I understand 
and support some specialized training requirements for therapies with 
psychedelics but it believe the state should consider the cost of these training 
requirements for busy and hard to ﬁnd masters level mental health professionals.  
Also, the state must consider whether these training requirements are consistent 
with training requirements for other types of specialized psychotherapies such as 
Dialectical Behavior Therapy, Trauma Focuses CBT, EMDR. It’s rare that other 
specialized psychotherapies are regulatory required to have additional training or 
certiﬁcation before oﬀering these interventions.  
Over all I believe that the New Mexico regulations have done a good job in 
balancing the need for expert medical screening and oversight and the need for 
access to professionals for the Certifying clinician and the Practitioner.     
  Henry T Harbin MD

From:

To the New Mexico Department of Health: 
I am a clinician in Smith, Arizona, I am writing to comment on proposed rule 7.35.3 NMAC ahead of the October 
2, 2026 hearing. 
To promote healing and wellness. 
I ask the department to make the following changes in the final rule. 
1. The property-owner statement, 7.35.3.11(B)(9). 
I ask the department to consider these solutions: 
a) Add to paragraph (B)(9) of 7.35.3.11: “This provision shall not apply to end-of-life patients or others seeking in-
home care, or to long-term care facility residents.” 
b) Delete paragraph (B)(9) of 7.35.3.11. If the department wants documentation of a home setting, follow Colorado 
here: the patient documents their own legal right to occupy the residence, no owner signs anything, and the clinician 
brings only the session dose and removes anything unused the same day. 
The landlord interest that justifies the cannabis provision, smoking on the property, is not present in a supervised 
psilocybin session administered by a licensed facilitator. Federal illegality alone does not justify it either: New 
Mexico has already chosen to depart from federal law on this. Giving landlords the power to block care for 
vulnerable and disabled patients is contrary to that choice, and leaves the department exposed under state protections 
against discrimination. A signed acknowledgment gives an owner nothing to gain and something to lose, so many 
will decline to sign, and care at home for anyone who does not own their home will end before they are able to 
receive treatment. 
2. The certifying clinician, 7.35.3.8 and 7.35.3.9(D). 
Remove the certifying clinician design. In its place, regulate around the specific contraindications, medical histories 
and medications that warrant clinical review. Where a risk factor is identified, require an attestation from a clinician 
whose scope of practice fits that risk that screening was conducted and benefits outweigh risks. 
The design exceeds the Act and misunderstands where the risk is. The most significant risks in psilocybin-assisted 
therapy are psychological, or medical risks that must be screened on the day of administration, such as intoxication. 
A one-time “prescriber” gateway misses them. It also creates a clinical encounter whose only purpose is program 
entry, which insurers will treat as lacking independent medical necessity, inviting claim denials and clawbacks. 
3. The patient application, 7.35.3.8. 
Strike the patient application process. 
It has no legal or policy justification in a supervised-use program. It adds administrative burden for the department, 
lengthens the wait for care, creates another store of patient data that can be accessed without consent or unlawfully 
taken, and further stigmatizes patients. 
Thank you for considering my comment. 
Pearish 
Smith, Arizona

===== PAGE 160 =====
Memo: Proposed NMAC 7.35.3 Provisions and Suggested Revisions 
Prepared for Legislative Counsel Bill Drafting 
Anne L. Metz, PhD, LPCC 
1. Certifying clinician / controlled substance registration requirement 
Citation: 
7.35.3.9(D)(2) 
Current text: 
Requires the certifying clinician’s application to include an "NM controlled substance number." 
Problem: 
The Medical Psilocybin Act defines "clinician" as any licensed provider authorized to diagnose and treat 
a qualifying condition; it does not require prescriptive authority. This requirement adds a credential the 
statute doesn’t call for, borrowed from the medical cannabis program, and excludes practitioners (LPCCs, 
psychologists, etc.) who are otherwise fully qualified under the Act to diagnose and treat these conditions. 
Suggested change: 
Strike the controlled-substance-number requirement from 7.35.3.9(D)(2). Eligibility to certify a patient 
should track the statutory definition of "clinician": any provider licensed to diagnose and treat the 
qualifying condition. If DOH wants a mechanism for flagging patients with specific pharmacological risk 
factors, that should be a separate, narrower referral trigger (see #4 below), not a blanket credentialing 
requirement on every certifying clinician. 
Senator Martin Hickey, MD additional comment: 
• 
I was one of the 2 chief co-sponsors of SB 219. In addition, I have been through a 5 day, 2 journey 
professional clinical psilocybin retreat. Given the regulations as proposed, I do not believe any of the 
regulators have experienced a clinical psilocybin journey/retreat.  As such they are limited in their insight 
and how the Medicine works. Instead, they seem to be replacing experienced based insight with medical 
cannabis regulations. They are totally different in criteria and benefit, and should be regulated as such. 
• 
The competencies of psilocybin clinicians are to understand who would benefit from psilocybin, do the 
initial screening and earning patient trust, recommend the appropriate dosage, conduct journeys and follow-
up integration. Education and certification must include 150 hours of specific training and experience in the 
therapeutic use of psilocybin. 
• 
A traditionally licensed health professional (MD, PA, NP etc.) without this training offers no value.  
• 
Further, there is NO call for a physical exam.  Rather, the initial screening and engagement should include 
an inquiry about medical/mental conditions and medications that psilocybin would exacerbate or be 
contraindicated for. If found, then a medical clearance should be required by a provider who has the 
documented competence on these. 
• 
Participation in a psilocybin-based treatment should be self-generated with as few bureaucratic barriers as 
possible.  I passed this concept in SB 273 in 2023 for mental health parity. Clinical psilocybin is about 
mental health. 
2. In-person examination requirement, narrow telehealth exceptions 
Citation: 
7.35.3.13(A)(1)–(2) 
Current text: 
Requires "an in-person physical evaluation and medical clearance" prior to certification, with 
telemedicine allowed only if the clinician (i) examined the patient in person within the prior six months, 
Exhibit 8.FFF.

===== PAGE 161 =====
(ii) reviewed records confirming an in-person diagnosis within six months, or (iii) formally consulted 
with a clinician who conducted that in-person exam. 
Problem: 
Combined with the credentialing requirement in #1, this creates a real access barrier, particularly in rural 
counties, since it multiplies how few providers can ever satisfy it. It also creates the same incentive 
structure that has made medical cannabis certifications a cash-pay, minutes-long transaction in New 
Mexico. Pill mills. 
Suggested change: 
If the certifying-clinician role is eliminated per #1, this section becomes unnecessary, since the 
practitioner already conducts an in-person intake as part of standard care. If DOH keeps a separate 
certifying step in some form, telehealth should be permitted on the same terms as the rest of New 
Mexico’s telehealth policy, without a six-month in-person anchor requirement. 
• 
Agree 
 
3. Patient application and 30-day departmental approval 
Citation: 
7.35.3.8(C) 
Current text: 
"Once the department has received a completed application, it will review the application and render a 
decision within 30 calendar days." 
Problem: 
This layers a non-clinical, discretionary state approval on top of a licensed clinician’s own determination 
that the patient qualifies. DOH has no clinical relationship with the patient and no information the 
certifying clinician/practitioner lacks. The people making these decisions also have no medical training. 
The 30-day window adds a substantial, medically unnecessary delay to care, particularly for patients with 
treatment-resistant or terminal conditions. 
Suggested change: 
Remove DOH’s individual approve/deny authority over patient applications. Replace it with a 
registration/notification requirement: the practitioner submits documentation of the patient’s qualifying 
diagnosis and screening for department recordkeeping and oversight purposes, and treatment may proceed 
on submission, not on approval. DOH retains full authority to audit, investigate, and take disciplinary 
action after the fact. 
• 
Agree 
• 
The state HAS NO ROLE in interfering with the patient provider relationship. This includes any approval!! 
4. Risk-based consultation, in place of the blanket certifying-clinician model 
Suggested new provision: 
Rather than requiring a separate certifying clinician for every patient, DOH should require the treating 
practitioner (who already meets the statutory definition of “clinician” and completes psilocybin-specific 
training) to obtain consultation from a prescriber or relevant specialist only when specific, enumerated 
risk factors are present, for example: uncontrolled hypertension, contraindicated medication regimens, or 
a personal or family history of psychosis. This directs medical expertise to the patients who actually need 

===== PAGE 162 =====
it and responds to risk dynamically rather than through a single fixed checkpoint applied to everyone 
regardless of risk. 
• 
Agree 
5. Landlord / property owner attestation for other approved locations 
Citation: 
7.35.3.11(B)(9) 
Current text: 
Requires "proof of ownership … or a signed, written statement from the owner of such property 
acknowledging that the owner understands that persons will be participating in the medical psilocybin 
program on the premises." 
Problem: 
Borrowed from the medical cannabis program, where the property-owner interest (odor, smoking, 
ventilation) is real. Psilocybin administration involves no smoking and no comparable property effect. As 
written, this could require a renting end-of-life or homebound patient to get landlord sign-off before 
receiving care in their own home. 
Suggested change: 
Add an exception: this requirement does not apply to a qualified patient’s own primary residence, to other 
patients receiving in-home care, or to long-term care facility residents.  
• 
Agree 
 
 
Respectively submitted: 
 
Martin Hickey, MD 
New Mexico State Senator 
Lead Co-Sponsor of 2026 SB

To Medical Psilocybin Act Board:
I am a New Mexico resident who works in the hospitality industry and has consulted
over the years to medical organizations and destination health resorts regarding
holistic and integrated medical modalities as well as published work on non-
pharmaceutical alternatives to treating depression, anxiety, substance addiction, less-
than optimal mental states and cognitive decline.
Familiar with the challenges experienced by Oregon and Colorado’s psilocybin
initiatives, New Mexico has a unique opportunity to create a program that really
benefits the community it seeks to serve and does not get compromised by excessive
regulatory structures and cost prohibitive licensing and regulatory fees. 
Below are my recommendations for 4 areas of concern:
     A patient who has already established mental health support with a professional
should not have to locate a separate medical prescriber to conduct a certification
evaluation. This seems unnecessary. How can a one-time consult with a stranger in
the medical field be qualified to provide an assessment? I would be curious on how
many medical prescribers would even want to participate in such a program, when a
licensed mental health professional already providing support to the patient is trained
to conduct assessments and diagnosis and determine if the patient meets the criteria
for psilocybin therapy.  
 
     With growing awareness of the benefits of end-of-life psilocybin care, I also
recommend the Department create an exemption for end-of-life patients receiving
medically necessary in-home care, and residents at long-term care settings.  I
recommend deleting property-owner requirements for approval to allow legal end-of-
life psilocybin care on a property. Considering this is such a sensitive time in an
individual’s life, when illness, mobility, transportation, and other factors create
additional challenges, end-of-life psilocybin care should not be burdened with
additional bureaucratic requirements because the patients do not own the property
Exhibit 8.GGG.

===== PAGE 164 =====
where they live.
 
     I hope New Mexico does not repeat the what happened in Oregon with initiatives to
establish excessive licensing fees and a pattern where the therapy is not financially
accessible to lower-income Oregonians and that clients are disproportionately
wealthy, often coming from out of state.
      https://www.klcc.org/health-medicine/2026-09-23/oregon-walks-back-fee-hikes-for-
psilocybin-therapy-providers
 
     My last comment concerns honoring centuries in plant medicine traditions with
indigenous cultures. I hope these rich traditions can be recognized and
representatives from indigenous cultures in New Mexico can participate in the
conversation on how psilocybin care can be provided affordably to at risk populations
within their communities.
  Wishing you and your associates a successful outcome in implementing this very
necessary program that can be of tremendous benefit.

To the New Mexico Department of Health:

to sign, and care at home for anyone who does not own their home will end before they are
able to receive treatment.

contraindications, medical histories and medications that warrant clinical review. Where a risk

To the New Mexico Department of Health:
I am a New Mexico resident in Santa Fe, and have been closely involved with the regulatory
committee this year. I am also a clinician and a landlord. I am writing to comment on
proposed rule 7.35.3 NMAC being considered atthe October 2, 2026 hearing.
In my work with with trauma and addictions for over 30 years, and more recently end of life
care, I’ve seen the desperate needs that are not currently being served. 
With the Medical Psilocybin Act,New Mexico has a chance to build an accessible system that
will serve as a model for other states. I appreciate the process that gives an opportunity for so
many well qualified contributors to develop our regulations. However, there are several
proposed items that could threaten all of our good intentions.
I ask the department to make the following changes in the final rule.
1. The property-owner statement, 7.35.3.11(B)(9). 
As a landlord, I would be happy to have my tenants receiving psilocybin Mushroom services
in their homes. However, I would find it distressing to have to sign a document to give them
permission– in essence, saying that I am aware that federally illegal activities are happening
on my property.
From a renters point of view, a requirement to contact landlords in this matter may sound
simple, but in reality, it could block access. I can’t think of any other precedent or landlords
are involved in sensitive medical treatments.
I support the addition to paragraph (B)(9) of 7.35.3.11: “This provision shall not apply to end-
of-life patients or others seeking in-home care, or to long-term care facility residents.”
The landlord interest that justifies the cannabis provision, smoking on the property, is not
present in a supervised psilocybin session administered by a licensed facilitator. Federal
illegality alone does not justify it either: New Mexico has already chosen to depart from
federal law on this. Giving landlords the power to block care for vulnerable and disabled
patients is contrary to that choice, and leaves the department exposed under state protections
against discrimination.
2. The certifying clinician, 7.35.3.8 and 7.35.3.9(D).
This is another area where we need to provide a direct and logical path to quality care. I join
my colleagues inrecommending removal of the certifying clinician design. In its place,
regulate around the specific contraindications, medical histories and medications that warrant
clinical review. Where a risk factor is identified, require an attestation from a clinician whose
scope of practice fits that risk that screening was conducted and benefits outweigh risks.
The design exceeds the Act and misunderstands where the risk is. The most significant risks in
psilocybin-assisted therapy are psychological, or medical risks that must be screened on the
Exhibit 8.III.

===== PAGE 169 =====
day of administration, such as intoxication. A one-time “prescriber” gateway misses them. It
also creates a clinical encounter whose only purpose is program entry, which insurers will
treat as lacking independent medical necessity, inviting claim denials and clawbacks.
3. The patient application, 7.35.3.8.
Strike the patient application process.
It has no legal or policy justification in a supervised-use program. It adds administrative
burden for the department, lengthens the wait for care, creates another store of patient data that
can be accessed without consent or unlawfully taken, and further stigmatizes patients.
Thank you for considering my comment. If I didn’t have another commitment, I would be at
the meeting and I’m sorry to miss it. I know my dedicated and knowledgeable colleagues will
show up and speak up, and trust that the Department of Health will agree with the good sense
of these recommendations.
