To the New Mexico Department of Health:
 I am a Professor at the UNM School of Medicine, and I am a board-certified
Family and Addiction Medicine Physician. My relevant roles include 
Codirector UNM research program for psychedelic assisted therapies, Medical
Director of UNM Milagro program for perinatal substance use,  member of New
Mexico Medical Psilocybin Program Advisory Board and 
Chair of   New Mexico Medical Psilocybin Advisory Board Committee on End-of-
life care
I have extensive training and experience in safely providing psychedelic assisted
therapy in our FDA approved research studies
I am writing to comment on proposed rule 7.35.3 NMAC ahead of the October
2, 2026 hearing. I ask the department to make the following changes:
Proposed NMC 7.35.3 Provisions and Suggested Revisions
1) Landlord / property owner attestation for other approved
locations
Citation:
7.35.3.11(B)(9)
Current text:
Requires "proof of ownership … or a signed, written statement from the owner
of such property acknowledging that the owner understands that persons will
be participating in the medical psilocybin program on the premises."
Exhibit 8.JJJ.

===== PAGE 171 =====
Problem:
As written, this could require a renting end-of-life or homebound patient to get
landlord sign-off before receiving care in their own home. Landlords may be
reluctant to sign-off as by doing so  they may actually increase their liability. The
sign-off process would require people facing end of life to share confidential
medical information. Colorado does not have any such requirement  for
landlord notification.
Suggested change:
Our rules and regulations should protect the ability of qualified patient to utilize
psilocybin assisted therapy  in their own home. The requirement for proof of
ownership should not include a patient’s own primary residence or  long-term
care facility residents.
2) Educational Requirements for Certifying Clinicians,
Practitioners, and Facilitators
Citation:
7.35.3.18
Current Text:
Requirements for initial practitioner and facilitator certification: All practitioners
and
facilitators shall complete a psilocybin therapy module consisting of a minimum
of 65 didactic hours with at least one third of the didactic hours conducted in
person, and 10 hours of simulated patient experience, prior to applying for
certification. 
Problem: The proposed rules and regulations require 75 didactic hours
compared to 150 in Colorado and 130 in Oregon, The decrease in the total
number of is reasonable given the increased number of required practicum
hours however to ensure adequate training for our patients there should be
specific requirements for certain areas of training. The DOH’s Advisory Board
training subcommittee recommended end of life committee recommends a
minimum of 8-10 hours of end-of-life training in recognition of the specific

===== PAGE 172 =====
issues that occur in therapy with end-of-life patients.  The DOH’s Advisory
Board training subcommittee recommended the follow minimum hour
requirements which were not included in the rules and regulations :
End-of-life and palliative care considerations 8 hours
Trauma and trauma-informed care     7 hours
Screening, suicidality, crisis response, and challenging experiences  5 hours
Suggested Change
Add specified minimum required  hours of training for the following 4 areas for
all licensed providers/practitioners and facilitators.
End-of-life and palliative care considerations 8 hours
Trauma and trauma-informed care     7 hours
Screening, suicidality, and crisis response 4 hours
Management of challenging experiences during and after psilocybin assisted
therapy  4 hours
3) Patient application and 30-day departmental approval 
Citation: 
7.35.3.8(C) 
Current text: 
"Once the department has received a completed application, it will review the
application and render a decision within 30 calendar days." 
Problem: 
This layers a non-clinical, discretionary state approval on top of a licensed
clinician’s own determination that the patient qualifies. DOH has no clinical
relationship with the patient and no information the certifying
clinician/practitioner lacks. The 30-day window adds a substantial, medically
unnecessary delay to care, particularly for patients with treatment-resistant or
terminal conditions. 

===== PAGE 173 =====
Suggested change: 
Remove DOH’s individual approve/deny authority over patient applications.
Replace it with a registration/notification requirement: the practitioner submits
documentation of the patient’s qualifying diagnosis and screening for
department recordkeeping and oversight purposes, and treatment may proceed
on submission, not on approval. DOH retains full authority to audit, investigate,
and take disciplinary action after the fact. The DOH or state should not add a
barrier that delays care and inappropriately interferes with the licensed
practitioner/client relationship
Sincerely
Larry Leenan MD MPH

[EXTERNAL] Additional Comment for the hearing today.

My name is Dr. Anne Metz, LPCC. I am a counselor in Taos County, and since 2022, I have
worked in both the Oregon and Colorado regulated access programs as an education
administrator and as a clinician. I have been involved in this rulemaking process since the
beginning.
From day one, the Department of Health determined that this program would look like the
Medical Cannabis Program, regardless of input from in-state and out-of-state experts to the
contrary. At every point, when the public and experts reached consensus on safety and
process, the Department met it with resistance and defaulted back to the medical cannabis
rules, copied almost verbatim from that program. Those rules were written for a different kind
of program. They are not appropriate for a treatment delivered in a supervised setting and
nowhere else.
The rules being finalized today do not follow the spirit of SB 219. We elected our state
legislators to pass laws on our behalf that reflect our values, and they did that in SB 219. The
executive branch has stepped in, claiming to know better and using the argument that
members of the public "can't write regulation language" as cover to copy and paste rules from
the Medical Cannabis Program. Public input was never meant to arrive as a finished regulatory
text. Turning that input into rules is the Department's job.
It is difficult to tell how much of this is a lack of effort on the part of the Department and how
much is executive overreach like we are seeing at the federal level. Either one fails the people
of New Mexico.
I hope the next administration is more receptive to the public and recognizes that the
legislature knew what it was doing when it drafted SB 219, and that the people involved in this
work know what they are talking about. The rules enacted should follow the spirit of the law,
and the Department should listen to the public and to the Advisory Board. The rules as
published fail on all three.
Respectfully submitted,
Dr. Anne Metz, LPCC

To the New Mexico Department of Health:
I am a New Mexico resident in Taos. I am writing to comment on proposed rule 7.35.3 NMAC ahead of the October
2, 2026 hearing.
I believe psilocybin therapy could benefit people in my community, and I am enrolled in psilocybin-facilitator
training in Colorado.
I ask the department to make the following changes in the final rule.
1. The certifying clinician, 7.35.3.8 and 7.35.3.9(D).
Remove the certifying clinician design. In its place, regulate around the specific contraindications, medical histories
and medications that warrant clinical review. Where a risk factor is identified, require an attestation from a clinician
whose scope of practice fits that risk that screening was conducted and benefits outweigh risks.
The design exceeds the Act and misunderstands where the risk is. The most significant risks in psilocybin-assisted
therapy are psychological, or medical risks that must be screened on the day of administration, such as intoxication.
A one-time “prescriber” gateway misses them. It also creates a clinical encounter whose only purpose is program
entry, which insurers will treat as lacking independent medical necessity, inviting claim denials and clawbacks.
2. The patient application, 7.35.3.8.
Strike the patient application process.
It has no legal or policy justification in a supervised-use program. It adds administrative burden for the department,
lengthens the wait for care, creates another store of patient data that can be accessed without consent or unlawfully
taken, and further stigmatizes patients. Lengthening the wait for care would harm patients, especially those with
treatment-resistant depression or those seeking end-of-life care.
Thank you for considering my comment.
Andrew Stuart
Taos, New Mexico

RE: Public Comment for 7.35.3 NMAC Concerning the New Mexico Medical Psilocybin 
Program (“Program”)  
  
Dear Mr. Jacob Clark and New Mexico Department of Health (“DOH”),   
  
Disability Rights New Mexico (“DRNM”) is New Mexico’s federally mandated Protection and 
Advocacy organization. DRNM’s mission is to protect, promote, and expand the rights of 
individuals with disabilities. As part of that mission, DRNM advocates for equitable laws, 
policies, and practices at the state and federal level which would provide individuals with 
disabilities equal opportunity to access and benefit from programs and services. Toward that end, 
DRNM provides these public comments regarding New Mexico’s Medical Psilocybin Program.  
 
DRNM understands those who may qualify for psilocybin treatment are individuals who have 
qualifying medical conditions such as: major treatment resistant depression, PTSD, substance 
use disorders, and end-of-life care.1 Because of DRNM’s engagement and advocacy on behalf of 
community members with disabilities, we also understand the profound impact that having a 
disability, such as the conditions listed above, can have on an individual and their ability to 
access programs, services, and treatment. Such conditions can have profound impacts on 
individuals’ abilities to engage in activities of daily living, and are additionally often among the 
most stigmatized diagnoses, thereby further limiting equal access to programs, services, and 
treatment.  
                                                          
1 https://www.nmhealth.org/about/mcpp/mpp/  
Exhibit 8.MMM.

===== PAGE 177 =====
2 
 
 
DRNM is pleased that New Mexico is among the first states to offer a medical psilocybin 
program. This treatment option offers potentially life-changing benefits to qualified patients. We 
also commend the Department of Health on embarking on the critical task of establishing safety 
guidelines, treatment protocols, and data collection to evaluate the Program’s effectiveness.2  
DRNM asserts that the evaluation on the Program’s effectiveness must include an assessment of 
patient accessibility and the removal of unnecessary barriers to participation in the Program. In 
order for the Program to be effective, it must be accessible to the population it was designed to 
serve. At the outset, the regulations, as currently drafted, erect unnecessary barriers for a 
particularly vulnerable subpopulation of eligible patients. Specifically, the Application Process 
for Healing Centers and Other Approved Locations (7.35.3.11 NMAC) includes provisions 
which are not only inaccessible, but would disproportionately exclude many of the patients the 
Program aims to treat by requiring written landlord approval. 7.35.3.11(B)(9) NMAC.  
  
The requirement of written landlord approval for in-home treatment under the Program 
undermines the purpose of the regulations and needlessly excludes many of the patients 
the Program was designed to assist. 
  
The scope of proposed 7.35.3.11 NMAC outlines the standards regarding the location in 
which treatment in the Program may be administered, including at healing centers and other 
approved locations. DRNM acknowledges and appreciates the Department’s efforts to ensure 
that individuals who qualify for the Program may experience immense benefit from receiving 
psilocybin treatment and therapy, and we endorse that determination that the administration of 
treatment may be provided in the home-setting as an “other approved location,” given the that 
many of our constituents require in-home care based on their medical conditions.  We endorse 
this rule proposed by DOH because we recognize the significant impact that complex and 
comorbid medical conditions may have on an individual and their ability to access care in an 
alternative, neutral setting outside of their home. 
 
Subsection B specifically addresses the requirements for “other approved locations” in 
which patients may participate in treatment under the Program. It is worth mentioning that the 
purpose of subsection B is to allow for the temporary certification for a location, such as a 
residence, to serve as a location in which treatment under the Program may be accessed by 
qualified patients who are “unable to be physically transported to a healing center, for other 
reasons of medical necessity, for purposes of improving patient access in rural and frontier 
counties, or to enable treatment to occur in a natural environment setting.” It is commendable 
that the Department has accounted for patients who are in such situations; however, the spirit of 
this allowance is undermined by the requirement that: 
 
Proof of ownership by the qualified patient or attending practitioner or facilitator 
of any property on which medical psilocybin administration sessions will be 
conducted, or a signed, written statement from the owner of such property 
acknowledging that the owner understands that persons will be participating in 
                                                          
2 Supra note 1. 
 

===== PAGE 178 =====
3 
 
the medical psilocybin program on the premises, and what those persons are 
authorized to do within the terms of their certification.  
7.35.3.11(B)(9) NMAC. (Emphasis added). 
 
This language effectively disqualifies many individuals from participating in the Program unless 
their landlord consents to their participation. Worse, it disproportionately impacts those would-
be participants in the Program who are the most in need of the treatment to be delivered in the 
home-setting. Approval for an “other approved location” already requires a heightened standard 
as the individual must be “unable to be physically transported to a healing center” including for 
medical necessity reasons, and seemingly geographical limitations (“for purposes of improving 
patient access in rural and frontier counties”). Landlord approval has nothing to do with whether 
or not an individual is unable to be physically transported to a healing center, nor does it serve 
any legitimate purpose to improving patient access in rural and frontier counties. Indeed, it is 
unclear what purpose this requirement serves. 
 
 
To be sure, the requirement for written landlord consent does not serve the interests of the 
Program, nor does it serve the interests of patients who would be participants of the Program. On 
its face, this requirement appears to serve the interests of landlords above the Program and 
patients. If this was the intention, it is misplaced. Arguably, it is likely never in the interests of a 
landlord to confirm in writing their assent to the use of a federally scheduled/illegal substance on 
their property. The needless exposure to liability on the part of the landlord will almost certainly 
result in most, if not all, landlords denying such requests. Further, there is no ascertainable 
interest which would support the landlord’s approval of a tenant participating in the Program. 
With no incentive to approve requests, and only potential liability in doing so, it is clear that the 
effect of this regulation will be the disproportionate denial of access to individuals with 
qualifying disabilities on the basis of property ownership rather than anything having to do with 
their medical needs. This regulation will have a discriminatory effect against those with the most 
need for the treatment in their home-setting. The requirement is problematic for several 
additional reasons.  
 
First, this requirement puts in the hands of landlords that which is a treatment decision. In 
no other sphere are landlords allowed such sweeping and encroaching authority to unilaterally 
deny patients access to in-home care.3 End-of-life hospice and in-home nursing care does not 
require landlord approval, even if those services require the administration of medication(s). Nor 
do a variety of in-home therapies, including occupational or physical therapy, Applied 
Behavioral Analysis (“ABA”) therapy, Eye Movement Desensitization and Reprocessing 
(“EMDR”) therapy, and many others. DOH should likewise not require landlord permission as a 
condition of accessing the medical psilocybin assisted therapy the Program offers.  
                                                          
3 While it is true that DOH has previously included landlord consent requirements for the medical marijuana 
program, there are important distinctions between the medical marijuana program and the medical psilocybin 
program. For instance, the medical marijuana program was a medical drug program in which participants obtained 
and dosed themselves with medicinal marijuana available for unaccompanied purchase. The medical psilocybin 
program is not a drug program, but rather a therapy program. Furthermore, participants in the medical psilocybin 
program do not obtain and dose themselves. Rather, psilocybin will be dosed and administered by a qualified 
professional who is responsible for maintaining a secure chain of custody. 7.35.3.14 NMAC. The programs are 
vastly different, and just because a landlord consent provision was utilized in the medical marijuana program does 
not mean that such a requirement is useful or appropriate for the medical psilocybin program. 

===== PAGE 179 =====
4 
 
Second, the terms of the regulation require a practitioner or facilitator to apply to the 
Department for the temporary certification of an “other approved location.” If the practitioner or 
facilitator is required to obtain written consent of a landlord before making such an application to 
the Department, then this regulation de facto requires the practitioner or facilitator to breach 
patient confidentiality and imposes upon them an affirmative requirement to violate their ethical 
duties to their patients by breaching confidentiality. DRNM urges DOH to acknowledge and 
consider that patients have a right to privacy and confidentiality. The Health Insurance 
Portability and Accountability Act of 1996, (“HIPAA”) is the federal law designed to afford and 
protect a patient’s right to keep their medical information private. The social values of HIPAA 
should control here and should arguably take precedence over a requirement for a landlord’s 
acknowledgment and approval of their tenant’s qualifying condition and participation in the 
Program. The removal of the proposed requirement of written landlord approval in order for 
tenants to receive medical psilocybin assisted therapy will foster and enhance the therapeutic 
environment clinicians aim to maintain while adhering to HIPAA requirements.   
 
Third, disclosures to a landlord of a tenant’s significant mental illness or other conditions, 
including the qualifying conditions to participate in the program, carries significant risk on the 
part of the tenant with a qualifying condition. Many individuals with qualifying conditions may 
find the requirement of the disclosure to the landlord to be an unacceptable risk due to personal 
and social stigmatization of serious mental illness and end-of-life scenarios, not to mention the 
stigmatization of the treatment modality itself. Thus, they are likely not to pursue the request in 
an effort to maintain their own confidentiality or out of fear of retaliation by the landlord. It is 
not unreasonable that someone who is engaged in end-of-life care might determine that their 
landlord would not renew their month-to-month lease if they knew they may expire on the 
property in the near future. Likewise, an individual with PTSD might determine that their 
landlord would not renew their lease if they knew the tenant carried such a diagnosis. There is 
simply no benefit to a patient’s landlord being privy to the tenant’s diagnosis, and disclosure of 
such only carries risk on the part of the tenant. 
 
Fourth, especially as it pertains to the stated purpose of “improving patient access in rural 
and frontier counties,” the requirement is likely to entrench the access problems it purports to 
address. The notion is that healing centers may not be geographically accessible to much of the 
state. In those instances, the only way to access the Program and treatment under the Program is 
through the “other approved locations” provisions of the regulations. Since most, if not all, 
landlords will not willingly expose themselves to unnecessary liability by approving 
participation in the Program in writing, this requirement will carry the effect of restricting 
patients to the geographical areas in which an approved healing center is located, thereby 
creating service deserts and pockets of inaccessibility throughout the state—precisely the effect 
the regulation purports to be attempting to address. 
 
Fifth, many New Mexicans participate in federal housing assistance programs, and 
DRNM’s constituents are often New Mexicans with disabilities with limited incomes. These 
individuals are particularly vulnerable from being excluded from the Program and treatment 
under the Program. Section 8 Housing, for example, is a federal program, and there is no 
recognized reasonable accommodation which would allow a Section 8 landlord to approve the 
use of a federally scheduled and illegal substance. Thus, the effect of this regulation on any New 

===== PAGE 180 =====
5 
 
Mexican participating in Section 8 who is unable to be transported to a healing center (for 
geographic access or medical necessity reasons) will be a de facto exclusion from participation in 
the Program on the basis of property ownership, economic means, and/or geographic location.  
This cannot be the intent of equitable access to the Program. 
 
Conclusion 
 
  
DRNM advocates and urges DOH to remove the language in 7.35.3.11(B)(9) NMAC in 
its entirety to ensure that DOH’s implementation of the Program removes unnecessary barriers 
and promotes equitable access to the Program by individuals with qualifying conditions.  
Individuals with disabilities, including those in frontier and rural areas of the state, must be 
afforded access to the benefits of the Program to the same extent as individuals who have the 
privilege and means to access treatment in the home they own or else in approved healing 
centers.  
 
DRNM is concerned that the proposed rule gives the authority to a landlord to determine 
whether or not a patient can receive medical psilocybin treatment and participate in the program, 
and we urge the Department to ensure that authority remains at the discretion of healthcare 
providers and treatment facilitators/practitioners in consultation with their patients. Populations 
who rely on public assistance and who are already subject to discriminatory and retaliatory 
practices, should not be exempt from treatment, nor be burdened with the risk of a denial or 
delay of services, because a landlord, whose job is to manage and own property, has been given 
the authority to decide how and if their tenants may access medical treatment.  
 
Individuals who require treatment for medical conditions embark on completing their 
own critical tasks in order to access programs and services that are meant to offer respite from 
the negative impacts of their disability and disability-related needs. However, as individuals 
attempt to access programs and services—which were perhaps initially designed to provide a 
benefit—often include inequitable policies and practices that become too burdensome and 
arduous to manage. Consequently, in efforts to access care, one’s disability can be exacerbated, 
medical conditions worsen, and participation in related services and programs remains elusive 
and inaccessible to those who most require flexibility. On behalf of our constituents, DRNM 
urges DOH to consider our concerns related to the proposed rulemaking found in 7.35.3.11(B)(9) 
NMAC, as we strongly believe that the proposed rule will actively disqualify individuals from 
the Program and preclude individuals who are unable to access a neutral location based on their 
disability and socioeconomic status. The requirement, if left unchanged, will amount to outright 
discrimination of individuals from the Program on the basis of their disability and economic 
status.4   
 
For all the foregoing reasons, DRNM urges DOH to reconsider final publication of the 
requirement under 7.35.3.11(B)(9).

EDITORIAL
Whitewashing psychedelics: racial equity in the emerging field of psychedelic-
assisted mental health research and treatment
Introduction
Growing research on potential therapeutic applications of highly
restricted
Schedule
I
hallucinogens,
including
the
‘classic
psychedelic’
psilocybin,
and
the
entactogen
3,
4-Methylenedioxymethamphetamine
(MDMA1),
has
demon-
strated notable promise for a range of mental health conditions
(Garcia-Romeu et al., 2016; Reiff et al., 2020). Preliminary
findings
suggest
substantial
benefits
of
psilocybin-assisted
treatment in major depression (Carhart-Harris et al., 2016;
Davis
et
al.,
2020),
alcohol
and
tobacco
dependence
(Bogenschutz et al., 2015; Johnson et al., 2014), and cancer-
related existential distress (Griffiths et al., 2016; Grob et al., 2011;
Ross et al., 2016). Similarly, MDMA-assisted therapy has demon-
strated persisting improvements in symptoms of post-traumatic
stress disorder (PTSD) (Mithoefer et al., 2019). As such, both
psilocybin and MDMA have been granted ‘breakthrough ther-
apy’ designation by the US Food and Drug Administration
(FDA), and pending positive outcomes of ongoing and upcom-
ing clinical trials, could emerge as a novel treatment paradigm
with considerable ramifications for mental healthcare (Reiff
et al., 2020).
Psychedelics and people of color
In the current issue, Williams et al. (2020) conducted a cross-
sectional online survey of 313 Black, Indigenous, and People
of Color (BIPOC) in North America, which suggests that natur-
alistic use of classic psychedelics or MDMA is associated with
significant reductions in traumatic stress, depression, and
anxiety symptoms related to experiences of racism. While
this observational study has some limitations, including reli-
ance on participant self-report and recall, and questions of
sample representativeness due to self-selection of partici-
pants, the study’s findings are noteworthy because they are
the first to our knowledge to focus exclusively on psychedelic
use outcomes in addressing racial trauma in a racial-ethnic-
ally diverse study sample. Pooled analyses of contemporary
clinical trials on psychedelics have found that white individu-
als comprise large majorities (>80%) of study participants,
substantially underrepresenting BIPOC, and calling into ques-
tion the generalizability of findings to non-white populations
and equity of access to such trials (Garcia-Romeu et al., 2021;
Michaels et al., 2018). Nationally representative data on past-
year lysergic acid diethylamide (LSD) use from 2015 to 2018
in the US suggest 71–74% of individuals who are using iden-
tify as non-Hispanic white, while only 13–16% identify as
Hispanic, and 4–5% as Black (Yockey et al., 2020). Data on
hallucinogen use (not including MDMA) in the US indicate
greatest prevalence of lifetime and past-year use among
Native American and white respondents, with lower preva-
lence among Hispanic, Black, and Asian individuals (Shalit
et al., 2019). The disparities in substance use patterns and
clinical trial enrollment, coupled with Williams et al. (2020)
findings on psychedelics’ impact on race-based trauma, raise
important questions regarding the sociocultural status of psy-
chedelics, systemic reasons for the underrepresentation of
BIPOC in this research, and the potential role of psychedelic-
assisted treatments in ameliorating mental health disparities
among diverse populations.
Systemic reasons for underrepresentation of minorities
in psychedelic research
The timeliness of Williams et al. (2020) findings cannot be
overstated in the current backdrop of racial and political
unrest in the U.S. Movements like Black Lives Matter (BLM)
have gained considerable momentum in calling attention to
systemic racism and police violence against Black and other
communities of color, and widespread race-based inequalities
(Jee-Lyn Garcıa & Sharif, 2015). However, the racial-ethnic div-
ide remains an ongoing and urgent affliction as evidenced
by substantial economic and health disparities (Williams,
2012). Given the promise of psychedelic-assisted treatments,
and the growing commercial interest in developing them, it
is imperative to consider how we as a field can ensure
research
on
psychedelic-assisted
therapies
is
conducted
equitably in diverse samples, and if approved, that these
treatments are accessible and beneficial to those commun-
ities most negatively impacted by structural inequities.
Multiple reasons for the lack of diversity and underrepre-
sentation of BIPOC as participants in psychedelic science
have been proposed in the literature. These include a lack of
cultural inclusivity and racial diversity within the research
community, stigma related to mental disorders and lack of
treatment access among BIPOC, recruitment methods that do
not emphasize recruitment of BIPOC, as well as differences in
attitudes and norms towards psychedelic use (Michaels et al.,
2018). In addition, we submit that larger historic and systemic
factors are at play. For Black Americans in particular, underre-
presentation in psychedelic research studies is likely related
to the history of racist and unethical research practices and
resulting mistrust in biomedical research institutions. High-
profile examples include the Tuskegee Syphilis study (Feagin
& Bennefield, 2014), wherein Black men were deceived
regarding their syphilis diagnosis and deprived of appropriate
treatment over the course of decades, or the case of
Henrietta Lacks, a Black woman whose cells were taken
DRUGS: EDUCATION, PREVENTION AND POLICY
2021, VOL. 28, NO. 3, 211–214
https://doi.org/10.1080/09687637.2021.1897331

===== PAGE 184 =====
without her consent while undergoing cancer treatment at
Johns Hopkins Hospital in 1951 and contributed to multiple
important medical discoveries (Skloot, 2011). On the basis of
such history, and the vulnerability inherent in high-dose psy-
chedelic administration, it is clear why many BIPOC might
look askance at participating in such studies today.
Unfortunately, underrepresentation of BIPOC remains a
problem not only for psychedelic research, but in clinical
research across the board (Nazha et al., 2019; Sardar et al.,
2014). Thus, rebuilding trust between research institutions
and BIPOC is vital to improving this situation, and an import-
ant factor therein is the presence of diverse medical and
research personnel, who also remain widely underrepre-
sented in the field (Cell Editorial Team, 2020; LaVeist et al.,
2003). Furthermore, the time-intensive nature of psychedelic-
assisted clinical trials, and their largely non-profit funding to
date, have made it challenging to provide financial incentive
for study participation, thus posing further issues for recruit-
ing
individuals
from
lower
socioeconomic
status
back-
grounds, who may be unable to take time away from work
and family responsibilities to participate in uncompensated
trials. With the median net worth of Black and Hispanic fami-
lies being less than 15% that of white families, BIPOC add-
itionally face substantial barriers to research participation in
psychedelic
trials
due
to
economic
inequity
(Dettling
et al., 2017).
Baltimore as a case study
The majority Black U.S. city of Baltimore, where both authors
reside, has profound health inequities between Black and
white residents, as well as between neighborhoods, and can
serve as a case study on the topics of racial inequity and psy-
chedelic research. Baltimore has a long history of racial
inequity, including the first municipal segregation ordinance
in the U.S. in 1911 (Power, 1983). Though this ordinance was
later deemed inadmissible by the U.S. Supreme Court in
1917, de facto segregation continued through denial of ren-
tal or sale of housing to Black individuals in established white
neighborhoods, or ‘redlining’ practices where certain (largely
poor and minority) neighborhoods were deemed high risk
and therefore residents were denied opportunities for invest-
ment and improvement (e.g. home loans) on that basis
(Pietila, 2010). The impact of redlining and housing discrimin-
ation continue to the present day. A widely publicized find-
ing of life expectancy by neighborhood has demonstrated
stark differences, as much as 20 years, between white affluent
neighborhoods, and impoverished Black neighborhoods, a
mere
5 mile
distance
from
each
other
(Marmot,
2017).
Moreover, ongoing tensions remain between Baltimore-based
Johns Hopkins University (JHU) and impoverished commun-
ities of color surrounding the Schools of Medicine and Public
Health (Baltimore City Health Department, 2017). Recent
examples include concerns over gentrification due to JHU’s
East Baltimore Neighborhood Development Initiative (Mitter,
2018) and the controversy over JHU’s plans to establish a pri-
vate police force (Burke, 2020).
Interestingly, Baltimore has long held a central role in psy-
chedelic research, with large-scale studies of LSD conducted
at the Spring Grove State Hospital reaching back to 1963,
including one of the few psychedelic research studies with a
majority Black sample examining the use of LSD treatment in
paroled men with a history of heroin dependence (Savage &
McCabe, 1973). More recently, the establishment of the Johns
Hopkins Center for Psychedelic and Consciousness Research
(CPCR) continues to build on this work in the Baltimore area.
However, tensions between JHU and the local community
likely contribute to difficulties recruiting Black participants
into research studies at the CPCR, underscoring a critical chal-
lenge to address going forward.
Poverty, psychedelic medicalization, and public
health impact
While increasing diversity among participants and personnel
in psychedelic research studies remain fundamental goals, it
is equally as important to note these treatments will not
solve all health disparities. Health services are responsible for
only a fraction of population health outcomes (McGinnis
et al., 2002). The story that emerges time and again is that
systemic inequalities, like poverty, have a far greater impact
on health and longevity, and no individual-focused treatment
approaches, even with promise as great as that of psyche-
delic therapies, will be able to rectify this situation.
Moreover, should psychedelics reach FDA approval status,
they will still require extensive healthcare infrastructure and
time to implement. Even with the growing enthusiasm
around the field, current medical training and treatment sys-
tems are unlikely to keep up with interest in psychedelics for
public health in the short term. These treatments will almost
certainly be expensive, difficult to access, and more easily
available to the financially well-off, like many cutting-edge
treatments. It is therefore crucial to develop pathways to
treatment for the less affluent, and BIPOC who already strug-
gle under the burden of significant health disparities. Work
by researchers such as Thomas et al. (2013) provides a not-
able example of community-based psychedelic treatment
approaches
in
Canadian
First
Nations
tribal
members.
Considering the long history of indigenous psychedelic use,
it is also important that medical adoption of psychedelics not
become another ‘discovery of America’ by colonizing forces,
but that these interventions be equitably accessible and
reciprocal in giving back to BIPOC communities where psy-
chedelic use has deep roots (George et al., 2019). Similarly,
from a social justice standpoint, the rapid commercialization
of formerly illegal substances for which many BIPOC have
faced criminal penalties, while wealthy investors come along
to profiteer, will require careful policy and regulatory rollout
to ensure appropriate parity. Finally, with decriminalization
initiatives around psychedelics gathering momentum (Feuer,
2020), it will be important not to create further disparities by
deeming some psychoactive substances primarily used in
white communities worthy of decriminalization, while keep-
ing others illegal.
212
J. THRUL AND A. GARCIA-ROMEU

===== PAGE 185 =====
Recommendations and conclusions
Williams et al. (2020) have made an important contribution
to the literature on the use of psychedelics and questions of
racial equity. Findings suggest that the use of psychedelics
has the potential to reduce symptoms of mental distress
experienced due to racism. While these findings should give
us some hope for the future, the research community has a
responsibility to make sure that promising psychedelic thera-
pies are equally effective and accessible to communities of
color. Research institutions must work on building trust with
BIPOC communities and address systemic and institutional
racism. Although psychedelics can potentially make a contri-
bution to reducing racial trauma, systemic interventions will
be needed to improve the lives of communities of color in
the U.S. Additionally, with increasing use of psychedelics
among bisexual individuals (Yockey et al., 2020), and severe
health disparities among sexual and gender minority groups
(Hsieh & Ruther, 2016; Streed et al., 2017), the issues raised
here will have to be expanded to include all people across
the spectrum of underrepresented backgrounds. Considering
the importance of the sense of unity or oneness that classic
psychedelics can evoke in mediating their long-term benefits,
the field should take this to heart in applying psychedelics
constructively towards ensuring equity of access and reduc-
ing health disparities, and make this an urgent priority.
Disclosure statement
Dr. Garcia-Romeu is a scientific advisor to Etha Natural Botanicals.

From: 
Whitney Joiner 
To: 
Clark, Jacob, DOH 
Subject: 
[EXTERNAL] Public comment on proposed 7.35.3 NMAC, October 2, 2026 hearing 
Date: 
Friday, October 2, 2026 8:16:03 AM 
[You don't often get email from whitneyj@mac.com. Learn why this is important at 
https://aka.ms/LearnAboutSenderIdentification ] 
CAUTION: This email originated outside of our organization. Exercise caution prior to clicking on links or opening 
attachments. 
To the New Mexico Department of Health: 
I am a New Mexico resident in Taos, NM, and I am also a clinician. I am writing to comment on proposed rule 
7.35.3 NMAC ahead of the October 2, 2026 hearing. 
I'm a licensed therapist in New Mexico and I hope to provide psilocybin-assisted care to New Mexicans. I'm 
concerned about access, availability and the need to take the realities of these modalities into account. I've benefited 
from psilocybin-assisted therapy as a patient and have some understanding of the specific and unique considerations 
this modality requires. 
I ask the department to make the following changes in the final rule. 
1. The property-owner statement, 7.35.3.11(B)(9). 
Add to paragraph (B)(9) of 7.35.3.11: “This provision shall not apply to end-of-life patients or others seeking in-
home care, or to long-term care facility residents.” 
The landlord interest that justifies the cannabis provision, smoking on the property, is not present in a supervised 
psilocybin session administered by a licensed facilitator. Federal illegality alone does not justify it either: New 
Mexico has already chosen to depart from federal law on this. Giving landlords the power to block care for 
vulnerable and disabled patients is contrary to that choice, and leaves the department exposed under state protections 
against discrimination. 
2. The certifying clinician, 7.35.3.8 and 7.35.3.9(D). 
Remove the certifying clinician design. In its place, regulate around the specific contraindications, medical histories 
and medications that warrant clinical review. Where a risk factor is identified, require an attestation from a clinician 
whose scope of practice fits that risk that screening was conducted and benefits outweigh risks. 
The design exceeds the Act and misunderstands where the risk is. The most significant risks in psilocybin-assisted 
therapy are psychological, or medical risks that must be screened on the day of administration, such as intoxication. 
A one-time “prescriber” gateway misses them. It also creates a clinical encounter whose only purpose is program 
entry, which insurers will treat as lacking independent medical necessity, inviting claim denials and clawbacks. 
3. The patient application, 7.35.3.8. 
Strike the patient application process. 
It has no legal or policy justification in a supervised-use program. It adds administrative burden for the department, 
lengthens the wait for care, creates another store of patient data that can be accessed without consent or unlawfully 
taken, and further stigmatizes patients. 
Thank you for considering my comment. 
Whitney Joiner, LMHC 
Taos, NM, New Mexico 
Exhibit 8.NNN.

To the New Mexico Department of Health: 
I am a New Mexico resident of Española and a renter. I am writing to comment on proposed 
rule 7.35.3 NMAC ahead of the October 2, 2026 hearing. 
I have trained and mentored clinical practitioners in alternative modalities and practices for 
over 10 years and have been involved in healing work for over 20 years. As a healing 
practitioner, my clients and community will be involved in receiving care. I have closely 
followed the systems in Oregon and Colorado and seen the issues with a lack of access. 
I ask the department to make the following changes in the final rule. 
1. The property-owner statement, 7.35.3.11(B)(9). 
Delete paragraph (B)(9) of 7.35.3.11. 
People should not have to ask permission from an outside person who can harm them by 
ending their lease agreement. Tenant rights are not strong in New Mexico. Many live on a 
month-to-month basis. Any person, not just one receiving end-of-life care, should be able to 
access this medicine in the comfort and safety of their own home without fear of retaliation or 
scrutiny. No one besides the practitioner and the client needs to know about this treatment, 
especially with stigma still being strong. This could keep people from receiving care, 
especially those who need it the most, because their home is the only safe or comfortable place 
for this type of treatment. A landlord should not be involved in anyone's medical care 
decisions. 
The landlord interest that justifies the cannabis provision, smoking on the property, is not 
present in a supervised psilocybin session administered by a licensed facilitator. A signed 
acknowledgment gives an owner nothing to gain and something to lose, so many will decline 
to sign, and care at home for anyone who does not own their home will end before they are 
able to receive treatment. 
2. The certifying clinician, 7.35.3.8 and 7.35.3.9(D). 
Remove the certifying clinician design. This keeps one from receiving care and does not make 
the process safer or better for anyone involved. People seeking this care already have enough 
medical hurdles to jump through. 
This will give insurers and practitioners not involved in this scope of work opportunity to deny 
or delay much needed care. 
3. The patient application, 7.35.3.8. 
Strike the patient application process. This is simply ridiculous red tape and will serve no one. 
Exhibit 8.OOO.

brings only the session dose and removes anything unused the same day.

The landlord interest that justifies the cannabis provision, smoking on the property, is not present in a supervised 
psilocybin session administered by a licensed facilitator. A signed acknowledgment gives an owner nothing to gain 
and something to lose, so many will decline to sign, and care at home for anyone who does not own their home will

and medications that warrant clinical review. Where a risk factor is identified, require an attestation from a clinician

From: 
Andra Elizabeth 
To: 
Clark, Jacob, DOH 
Subject: 
[EXTERNAL] Public Comment on Proposed Rule 7.35.3 NMAC and Amendments to 7.35.2 NMAC 
Date: 
Friday, October 2, 2026 9:59:47 AM 
You don't often get email from airesdra@gmail.com. Learn why this is important 
CAUTION: This email originated outside of our organization. Exercise caution prior to 
clicking on links or opening attachments. 
I write to urge the Department to reduce the administrative barriers in proposed 7.35.3 
NMAC. As written, the rule will limit medical psilocybin to patients with money, stable 
housing, reliable internet, and the ability to travel, and will fall hardest on rural and lower-
income New Mexicans, many of whom most desperately need care. 
My experience 
I was diagnosed with Complex PTSD in 2019 after surviving an abusive childhood, a story 
shared by far too many New Mexicans. This condition led me to attempt to end my life more 
than once. In 2023, I needed months of intensive care I could not find in New Mexico. I was 
fortunate to have the means to leave the state for treatment. Even then, I continued to struggle 
until I found psychedelic-assisted therapy. 
People often come to psilocybin in their darkest days, when getting out of bed is almost more 
than they can manage. Every added step is a barrier someone in crisis may not survive. 
Specific concerns 
I am not an expert, and I ask the Department to give serious weight to the experts who spoke 
at today's hearing. Their concerns, which I share, include: 
1. Landlord approval (7.35.3.11(A)(8) and (B)(9)). Requiring a property owner's signed 
acknowledgment forces renters to disclose their mental health treatment to a landlord, 
risking their privacy and housing. Homeowners face no such burden, creating a two-tier 
system based on wealth. 
2. Patient enrollment and screening (7.35.3.8 and 7.35.3.13(A)). Requiring an in-person 
exam within six months, even for telemedicine certification, means long drives for rural 
patients. The online-only application, multiple attestations, 30-day review, and two-year 
re-enrollment add steps for people least able to manage them. I ask the Department to 
allow full telehealth certification and offer phone and paper enrollment options. 
3. Other approved locations (7.35.3.11(B) and (D)). The rule itself names these sites as a 
way to improve access in rural and frontier counties, yet each requires a lengthy 
application and expires after only 90 days. Certification should match the patient's 
enrollment period. 
4. Overregulation of healing centers (7.35.3.11(A) and 7.35.3.20). More than two dozen 
application items, strict group staffing ratios, two wilderness-certified staff for sites 15 
or more minutes from emergency services, and two-day reporting of "challenging 
psychological, emotional, or behavioral reactions," which are often an expected part of 
therapy, will raise costs and discourage centers from opening outside urban areas. 
Exhibit 8.QQQ.

===== PAGE 193 =====
 
Requirements should scale to the size and setting of the center. 
5. Appeals (7.35.3.27). Requiring hearing requests by certified mail and in-person 
hearings in Santa Fe for anyone within 100 miles adds cost and travel for patients. 
Electronic submission and remote hearings should be the default. 
Closing 
I am proud that New Mexico is leading the nation on access to this life-saving treatment. I 
urge the Department to listen to today's expert testimony and remove these unnecessary 
barriers so this program closes our state's mental health care gaps instead of widening them. 
Lives depend on it. 
Respectfully submitted, 
Andra Elizabeth 
Albuquerque, New Mexico 
Phone: 505-504-2695 
Email: airesdra@gmail.com

Hi, I would encourage caution before approving the psilocybin program. 
I know that there are a lot of comparisons to the marijuana industry but I’d encourage you to 
also consider some of the issues with the ketamine clinics in the state. 
My main concern is that there isn’t an integrated approach. As with ketamine, people can go in 
for services and get approved by the doctor, but I don’t believe there is a requirement to offer 
therapy afterwards. I feel that the diagnosis should be provided by someone with less of a 
financial interest, and some kind of talk therapy should be more strongly encouraged with or 
as a part of the program. You’ll see more successful outcomes and less abuse of the system. 
Thanks 
Mike Courtois 
Albuquerque

Hi Jacob, 
I am commenting as a citizen and resident of Santa Fe, New Mexico. I am a Licensed Clinical 
Social Worker in New Mexico and Colorado, and I also a Licensed Natural Medicine Clinical 
Facilitator in Colorado since January of 2025. I completed my Psilocybin Facilitator Certification 
at Innertrek in Portland, Oregon in December of 2024. 
I would additionally like to offer a comment regarding the education and practicum requirements of 
practitioners and facilitators. Most educational programs for psilocybin facilitation cost upwards of 
$10,000. I run a 96-hour Psychedelic Studies curriculum that is a Continuing Education program 
located in Santa Fe New Mexico at Southwestern College. This is a Community Education 
program and is open to counselors in training, licensed counselors, as well as citizens who wish to 
educate them to the psychedelics. Therefore it would be an excellent way to train the non-clinical 
facilitators in the program. 
I weave ethics through each of my seven course modules (each of which are 16 hours over a 
weekend). My focus is to support counselors and coaches to develop an ethical self-reflective 
framework and mindset and professional council of peers to consult with -- my goal has always 
been the hope that by educating providers in psychedelic ethics through all of my subjects 
(history, ethics, neuroscience, phenomenology, Grof breathwork, and working with special 
populations (substance use, veterans/first-responders, and end of life care) we create a more 
informed provider pool and by doing so, we also inform the public around the safe and ethical use 
of psychedelics--an informed public is a safer public. 
Cost: Each of my classes are very low cost, at only $375/per class (and each class is 16 hours) 
(note that the Grof Breathwork class is slightly more expensive at $585 as we have guest 
teachers and an offsite location). I always invite local speakers, Indigenous knowledge holders, 
and others who have been facilitating with or teaching about psychedelics in the field. If one were 
to go through all of my classes and achieve a certificate (96 hours of curriculum) they would pay 
close to $2296 (much less than most facilitator programs). Since the educational requirements of 
the proposed rules is less (in the 70+ hours) than my 96 hour program, it is my hope that New 
Mexicans will contribute to providing the education to our local practitioners and facilitators. 
Thank you for the opportunity to offer my comment.

To the New Mexico Department of Health: 
I am a New Mexico resident in Albuquerque. I am writing to comment on proposed rule 7.35.3 NMAC ahead of the 
October 2, 2026 hearing. 
As an educator, practitioner and citizen this matters 
I ask the department to make the following changes in the final rule. 
1. The property-owner statement, 7.35.3.11(B)(9). 
Delete paragraph (B)(9) of 7.35.3.11. 
The landlord interest that justifies the cannabis provision, smoking on the property, is not present in a supervised 
psilocybin session administered by a licensed facilitator. A signed acknowledgment gives an owner nothing to gain 
and something to lose, so many will decline to sign, and care at home for anyone who does not own their home will 
end before they are able to receive treatment. 
2. The certifying clinician, 7.35.3.8 and 7.35.3.9(D). 
Remove the certifying clinician design. In its place, regulate around the specific contraindications, medical histories 
and medications that warrant clinical review. Where a risk factor is identified, require an attestation from a clinician 
whose scope of practice fits that risk that screening was conducted and benefits outweigh risks. 
The design exceeds the Act and misunderstands where the risk is. The most significant risks in psilocybin-assisted 
therapy are psychological, or medical risks that must be screened on the day of administration, such as intoxication. 
A one-time “prescriber” gateway misses them. It also creates a clinical encounter whose only purpose is program 
entry, which insurers will treat as lacking independent medical necessity, inviting claim denials and clawbacks. 
3. The patient application, 7.35.3.8. 
Strike the patient application process. 
It has no legal or policy justification in a supervised-use program. It adds administrative burden for the department, 
lengthens the wait for care, creates another store of patient data that can be accessed without consent or unlawfully 
taken, and further stigmatizes patients. 
Thank you for considering my comment. 
JoEllen Schimmels 
Albuquerque, New Mexico 
Sent from my iPhone

Dear Administrator Clark, 
I am writing to provide public comment regarding New Mexico's developing regulations. I 
strongly encourage the Department of Health to consider aligning our rules with the 
framework implemented by Colorado for psilocybin, rather than adopting the lengthy, 
complex procedures modeled after New Mexico's medical cannabis program. 
Colorado's approach allows the credentialed practitioner delivering the treatment to directly 
manage and take ownership of the process. This streamlines the procedural burden 
significantly while maintaining public health oversight. Replicating the highly administrative 
medical cannabis model in New Mexico would create unnecessary barriers to access and delay 
treatments that could be handled efficiently at the provider level. 
Thank you for your time, leadership, and consideration of this administrative framework. 
Sincerely, 
Meredith Vaughn

clicking on links or opening attachments. 
Dear Jacob, 
Please include the following comments in the written community feedback to the Psilocybin Rules hearing from today (7.35.3 
NMAC, concerning the New Mexico Medical Psilocybin Program): 
There are three things we need to consider with this regulation: access, practicality, and most importantly, affordability. Many 
of the centers in Oregon are shutting down and low or even middle income people are unable to afford psilocybin services 
there because there has been unnecessary and costly oversight that has made the treatment financially untenable for most. We 
literally cannot afford to make the same mistakes here in New Mexico. 
I agree with Denali Wilson that we should remove state-level screening/ approval process (7.35.3.8 B I) of patients prior to 
administration. If we properly train our psilocybin clinicians, we should be able to rely on them to screen and determine 
eligibility for psilocybin treatment or add extra screening only when certain medical requirements are met. Maintaining this 
state-level screening processes will add unnecessary stress, time, but most importantly cost, and can be potentially 
demoralizing or inappropriate. We do not want to create a system that will spend unnecessary money on red-tape that could 
potentially bar patients from receiving care that they need and that their clinicians agree will benefit them. We do not need to 
have a system that mirrors a third-party insurance gate-keeping. 
I believe some of the regulations about the number of clinicians or facilitators in the room are unnecessarily cautious and will 
contribute to the failure of the program due to financial impracticality. I believe we should drop the requirement that both a 
facility and a practitioner need to be in the room for an individual session(7.35.3.20.H.5), it will make it too expensive and in 
many cases for people with PTSD is more appropriate to start then with a small dose, making this unnecessary. An alternative 
would be to require a second person or a practitioner in the facility at that time (especially if the patient is a high risk) or 
easily accessible by phone. 
Additionally 7.35.3.20.K requires two people to have Wilderness medical or EMT training in rural settings. I believe one 
person could easily fulfill this requirement and two will make it too costly. 
I believe some adjustments need to be made to 7.35.3.20.H.5. Group administration has many benefits including community 
supports, but not the least of which is it supports financial practicality. I am looking at creating a healing center that would use 
the group model and invite participants for one week retreats in which they underwent two dosing sessions. We thought three 
or four clinicians to one patient would be a good ratio and allow for the cost of one-week retreats to stay under $5,000 as 
facilitator or practitioner time is the most costly element of this treatment. As of now, this rule states: “group administration 
sessions a minimum of one practitioner for every eight patients and a minimum of one facilitator or qualified student for every 
two patients”. The “And” here means if you have an 8-person group, you need 5 people helping (1 practitioner and 4 
facilitators) or in a 12 person group you need 8 helpers. This ratio will make these retreats prohibitively expensive, and we 
could end up exceeding Oregon pricing, costing participants $8-10,000 or more for a retreat. This seems especially 
inappropriate when many clients need to start at a small dose to feel safe with the process. While I want to make sure all 
clients are supported, I think adjusting the ratios for groups will make this treatment more practical. 
I agree wholeheartedly with Jeff Steinorn that we should remove the landlord approval requirements for both in-home use 
(7.35.3.11. A 9) and use in healing center rental properties (7.35.3.11 A 8). Other medical practitioners are not asking their 
landlord’s approval to do medical procedures or administer medications in their offices. This is both inappropriate and 
potentially exposes sensitive client information to the landlords or people in the adjacent area. It could result in clients being 
harassed or exposed. It could also impede small businesses from opening in rural areas where the care may be needed the 
most. 
Regarding 7.35.3.8 B, are in-person assessments alway necessary prior to dosing sessions? This will really impede access to 
Exhibit 8.XXX.

===== PAGE 203 =====
rural and indigenous communities where transportation is inaccessible or very expensive (the price of gas is continuing to rise 
and many of this communities may be hundreds of miles from our larger cities). Medical screening like blood pressure 
measurements can be provided by a different provider or in-person the day of the session rather than requiring a separate in-
person session. 
I agree with some of the voices from today that there should be a path for healers from indigenous traditions that are working 
outside of the western clinical model to participate as facilitators or practitioners. 
Thank you, 
Maria Fini, PhD
